## Daniela Galatro

**Affiliation:** Department of Mechanical and Industrial Engineering, University of Toronto. Cross-collaborative with the Edward S. Rogers Sr. Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering (Trescases) and the broader UTEV (University of Toronto Electric Vehicle) Research Centre.

**Lab focus (one sentence):** **Inferred** from Article 1 + the consistent co-author cluster (Da Silva, Romero, Trescases, Amon) across her recent publications: data-based and statistical degradation modelling of lithium-ion batteries for electric-vehicle and second-life applications, with emphasis on cell-to-cell variation, accelerated-aging test design, and battery thermal / management system implications. **Verify against the lab's current publications and webpage before treating this as authoritative.**

**Lab webpage / Scholar:** Not yet provided — needs to be sourced. Email on file (Article 1): daniela.galatro@mail.utoronto.ca.

### Article 1: Challenges in Data-Based Degradation Models for Lithium-Ion Batteries

- **Full citation:** **Galatro, D.**, Da Silva, C., Romero, D. A., Trescases, O., & Amon, C. H. (2020). Challenges in data-based degradation models for lithium-ion batteries. *International Journal of Energy Research*, 44(5), 3954–3975. DOI: 10.1002/er.5196
- **Authorship caveat:** Galatro is **first author and sole corresponding author** — i.e., this is a **flagship lab-signal paper** for advisor evaluation, not a middle-authored contribution. Co-authors are her established U of T cluster (Da Silva, Romero, Trescases, Amon). **Strongest evidence yet that this professor would directly supervise the kind of work this section scopes.**
- **Problem in plain English:** Battery aging in EV applications is usually fit with an empirical formula `C(t) = C0·(1 − kc·t^α)`, where `kc` is a stress-dependent kinetic constant and `α` is a time-exponent. The dominant recipe in the field (Mathieu et al. 2017) treats `α` as a single averaged constant (~0.745) and fits `kc` as a multi-factor quadratic of temperature, SOC, charge-rate, and discharge-rate. Galatro applies that recipe to four published datasets covering **LMO/NMC, NCA, NMC, and LFP** chemistries and shows the assumption breaks: `α` actually varies from ~0.20 to ~1.66 across tests and depends on chemistry, SOC, C-rate, and (for LFP) temperature. The paper diagnoses the failure modes, proposes regrouping `α` by SOC band (cuts max NMC error from 11.0 % to 2.8 %), and prescribes a corrected accelerated-test matrix and three-step modelling methodology.
- **ML task type (REFRAMED — paper itself contains no ML):** The paper is purely statistical regression (linearised Dakin model fit by IRLS + M-estimators in R; quadratic regression for `ln(kc)` vs. stress factors). The natural ML reframing is **multi-stress-factor regression of capacity fade**: input = (time / Ah-throughput, temperature, SOC, C-rate, DOD, chemistry label) → output = capacity retention (% of nominal). Cleanest concrete task: regression of capacity fade from per-cell stress-factor vectors, with optional uncertainty quantification (GP) or active learning (Bayesian DOE).
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality: **Tabular** — accelerated-aging test conditions (T °C, SOC %, charge-C-rate, discharge-C-rate, DOD %, time in days or Ah-throughput, chemistry one-hot or categorical), with periodic capacity-fade measurements as the time series.
  - Shape/size: ~150–200 (test × time-point) rows pooled across the four datasets (Mathieu/MOBICUS baseline 27 tests; Wikner 21 tests; Keil 2016 NCA/NMC/LFP at 48 calendar tests each).
  - Preprocessing in the paper: Linearisation via `ln[−ln(C(t)/C0)] = ln(kc) + α·ln(t)` (Eq. 6); IRLS + M-estimators to robust-fit per-test `(kc, α)`; quadratic regression of `ln(kc)` against `{1/T, SOC, IC, ID, 1/T², SOC², cross-terms}` (Eq. 7) and a reduced 9-coefficient version (Eq. 8).
- **Output representation:**
  - What the model predicts: Capacity retention (%) at any time t, given stress-factor vector and chemistry; equivalently, time-to-failure for any chosen capacity-fade threshold.
  - Units / label space: Continuous regression in [0, 100] %; secondary regression on `(kc, α)` in log space.
- **Model architecture (paper has none — this is a suggestion):** Replace the parametric Dakin + quadratic-`kc` pipeline with a learned regressor — gradient-boosted trees (LightGBM / XGBoost) for a strong baseline, plus a Gaussian process (scikit-learn / GPyTorch) for principled uncertainty bounds, and optionally a small physics-informed MLP (PyTorch) that retains the `t^α` factor as an inductive bias.
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: Four datasets — (1) **Mathieu et al. 2017 / MOBICUS** baseline (LMO/NMC, 43 Ah prismatic, 27 calendar+cycling tests); (2) **Wikner 2017 thesis** (Chalmers; LMO/NMC pouch, 26 Ah, 21 tests); (3) **Keil et al. 2016** *J. Electrochem. Soc.* (NCA cylindrical 2.8 Ah, NMC cylindrical 2.05 Ah, LFP cylindrical 1.1 Ah; 48 calendar tests each across 25/40/50 °C × 16 SOC levels × 300 days).
  - Size: ~144 calendar tests + ~50 cycling tests across all four datasets — small by ML standards.
  - Source: Three published academic studies; raw values mostly accessible via the source papers' tables.
  - Public? **Partial.** Keil 2016 calendar data is fully tabulated in the JES paper; Wikner 2017 is a Chalmers thesis (raw data in appendices); Mathieu/MOBICUS is partially in the source paper plus Ben-Marzouk MOBICUS WEVJ paper.
  - Link: Keil 2016 → https://doi.org/10.1149/2.0411609jes ; Mathieu 2017 → https://doi.org/10.1016/j.energy.2017.11.130 ; Wikner 2017 → Chalmers thesis repository (URL to be confirmed). **No code or unified data deposit released for the Galatro paper itself.**
- **Reported headline metric:** `α` ranges 0.20–1.66 across tests (vs. the assumed-constant 0.745). Full quadratic model R² = 0.945–0.993 per chemistry; reduced model R² = 0.888–0.993. **Regrouping `α` by SOC band reduces maximum relative error in NMC (Dataset III) from 11.03 % → 2.82 %** at 500-day capacity prediction. Linear-regression of `α` vs. stress factors: significant SOC dependence (p < 0.05) for all chemistries in calendar mode; significant C-rate dependence in cycling mode; significant temperature dependence only for LFP. Sample-size / learning-curve analysis: linear-T effect's p-value falls below 0.05 at ~23 tests; quadratic effects similarly stabilise around 22 samples.
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**
  - **Keil et al. 2016 NCA/NMC/LFP calendar dataset** — 48 calendar tests × 3 chemistries — match: **direct (paper uses it)** — access: medium (extract from JES paper tables; needs digitisation) — https://doi.org/10.1149/2.0411609jes
  - **NASA PCoE Battery Aging dataset** — 18650 LCO cells, randomised usage profiles, multiple aging conditions — match: close — access: easy (NASA Open Data Portal, direct download) — https://www.nasa.gov/intelligent-systems-division/discovery-and-systems-health/pcoe/pcoe-data-set-repository/
  - **CALCE (U Maryland) Battery Group datasets** — multiple chemistries (NMC, LCO, LFP), well-documented calendar + cycling — match: close — access: easy (web download, free) — https://calce.umd.edu/battery-data
  - **Severson et al. 2019** (MIT/Stanford) — 124 LFP fast-charging cells, well-curated — match: loose (cycling-only, fast-charge focus, but the cleanest large public Li-ion dataset for ML benchmarking) — access: easy — https://data.matr.io/1/
  - **Battery Archive aggregator** — multiple chemistries unified under one schema — match: close — access: easy — https://www.batteryarchive.org/
- **Data access difficulty (for the paper's own dataset):** **Medium** — the four cited datasets are public but require manual assembly: Keil 2016 needs table digitisation from the JES PDF, Wikner 2017 needs thesis-appendix extraction, MOBICUS needs cross-referencing with Ben-Marzouk's WEVJ paper. The Galatro paper itself releases no consolidated CSV.
- **Tooling I would need to learn:** Standard ML stack — pandas, scikit-learn, LightGBM / XGBoost, optional GPyTorch (or scikit-learn GPs) for uncertainty, optional small PyTorch MLP for the physics-informed variant. Optional R if mirroring the paper's IRLS + M-estimator pipeline. **No domain-specific ML libraries** (no RDKit, no GNN frameworks, no pymatgen, no protein language models) — this is the most tooling-friendly article in the survey so far.
- **Portfolio angle — what a good GitHub project based on this would look like:** A clean three-notebook benchmark: (1) **Reproduce Galatro's Dakin + quadratic-kc pipeline** on the Keil 2016 NCA/NMC/LFP calendar dataset, recovering α-spread per chemistry and the SOC-band regrouping result. (2) **Replace the parametric pipeline with a learned regressor** — LightGBM baseline + Gaussian Process for principled uncertainty bounds — and show the ML model captures `α`-variation across SOC bands without manual regrouping. (3) **Cross-chemistry transfer** — train jointly on three of the four chemistries, evaluate held-out performance on the fourth. The README's headline claim: *"A learned regressor matches Galatro's hand-engineered SOC-band pipeline on Keil 2016 with no manual feature segmentation, and provides calibrated uncertainty bounds suitable for downstream battery-management-system design."* Distinct from any prior portfolio piece in the survey because it's **tabular-only, laptop-tractable, and maps onto a recognised industrial ML task** (RUL / SOH prediction for EVs and grid storage).

#### Article 1 ranking

|---|---|---|
| Data availability | 7 | Keil 2016 NCA/NMC/LFP calendar dataset is fully tabulated in the JES paper; NASA PCoE, CALCE, Severson 2019 provide additional public Li-ion aging benchmarks. The paper's own four-dataset compilation is published but not unified |
| Data access ease | 5 | Keil 2016 must be extracted from JES tables; Wikner 2017 from a Chalmers thesis; MOBICUS partly from a WEVJ companion paper. NASA / CALCE alternatives are easier (one-click downloads). Not a one-line `pip install`-and-load situation |

**Article 1 verdict:** A **credible and unusually tooling-friendly** GitHub project is achievable from this paper — pure tabular regression on a small public dataset, runs on a laptop, uses only the standard ML stack, and maps onto a recognised industrial task (battery RUL / SOH prediction). The main costs are (i) data assembly from three separate source papers and (ii) the fact that the paper itself contains no ML, so the student is inventing the supervised task rather than competing against an established benchmark.

### Article 2: Modeling Degradation of Lithium-Ion Batteries Considering Cell-to-Cell Variations

- **Full citation:** **Galatro, D.**, Romero, D. A., Freitez, J. A., Da Silva, C., Trescases, O., & Amon, C. H. (2021). Modeling degradation of lithium-ion batteries considering cell-to-cell variations. *Journal of Energy Storage*, 44, 103478. DOI: 10.1016/j.est.2021.103478
- **Authorship caveat:** Galatro is again **first author and sole corresponding author** — same lab cluster as Article 1 (Romero, Da Silva, Trescases, Amon) plus Freitez at UNEXPO (Venezuela). **Second strong lab-signal paper** — pattern across Articles 1 and 2 confirms this is Galatro's independent research direction.
- **Problem in plain English:** Battery packs (e.g., Tesla S = 7104 cells in a 74p6s × 16 architecture) age unevenly. "Cell spreading" comes from (1) intrinsic manufacturing variability between cells and (2) uneven exposure to stress factors — especially temperature gradients across the pack. The paper proposes a **statistical model that predicts each cell's capacity-fade trajectory and rolls those up to module/pack-level capacity fade or time-to-failure**, while explicitly capturing the spreading. This is the natural follow-up to Article 1's "challenges" critique — Article 1 dissects why averaged-α regression breaks; Article 2 proposes a stochastic-process replacement.
- **ML task type (REFRAMED — paper itself contains no learned ML):** A **3-parameter non-homogeneous Gamma process (NHGP)** is fit to per-cell capacity-fade-vs-time data via maximum-likelihood estimation, with goodness-of-fit checked by KS and Gamma-variance tests. Pack rollup is done by **Monte Carlo simulation** with **sum() (parallel)** and **min() (series)** compositions of Gamma-distributed degradation increments. An **Arrhenius acceleration factor** is plugged in to adjust trajectories under spatial / temporal temperature gradients. A **cell-sampling strategy (SSPE metric)** estimates the minimum number of cells whose capacity must be measured to bound pack-level estimation error. Natural ML reframing: **probabilistic time-series regression for capacity fade / RUL** — replace the NHGP closed form with a learned probabilistic model (Bayesian neural net, deep ensemble, or GP over time × stress factors) and benchmark head-to-head.
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality: **Tabular per-cell time series** — capacity (% of nominal) at known cycle/day points + cell temperature where temperature gradients are imposed.
  - Shape/size: 14 cells × ~11 timestamps over 1102 days (the reference dataset); plus simulated packs of 20–80 cells × 16 modules × 6 sets for the Tesla-S architecture.
  - Preprocessing in the paper: NHGP closed-form fitting via MLE with `φ(t) = t^c` time transformation; KS / Gamma-variance GOF tests; Arrhenius acceleration factor `AF = exp[(−1547.5 / R) · (1/T_ref − 1/T)]` (Eq. 8).
- **Output representation:**
  - What the model predicts: Capacity fade (%) at any time t, OR time-to-failure (days) at any chosen capacity-fade threshold (commonly 20 % EOL); both with full Gamma-process uncertainty bounds.
  - Units / label space: Continuous regression with calibrated predictive distribution (Gamma at the cell level; pack-level distribution learned by Monte Carlo).
- **Model architecture (paper itself uses no ML):** Three-parameter NHGP (`a`, `b`, `c` per Eq. 2) per cell + per pool; Monte Carlo simulation for pack composition. Suggested ML replacement: probabilistic deep regressor (deep ensemble, Bayesian NN, or GPyTorch GP over `(time, T, SOC, ...)`) + analogous Monte Carlo rollup.
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: **Reference dataset = Baumhöfer et al. 2014** — 14 cells from a batch of 51 commercial **2.8 Ah 18650 cylindrical** cells with **60 % LCO / 40 % NMC** cathode, cycled 1000 times at 25 °C, charge C/2, discharge 1.5 C, capacity tracked over 1102 days at 11 timestamps.
  - Size: ~14 cells × 11 timestamps = ~154 raw datapoints; Monte Carlo simulations expand this to thousands of synthetic trajectories.
  - Source: Baumhöfer, T., Brühl, M., Rothgang, S., & Sauer, D. U. (2014). *Journal of Power Sources*, 247, 332–338.
  - Public? **Yes** (single source paper). Raw values appear in Fig. 3 of the source paper; supplementary material likely has tables.
  - Link: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jpowsour.2013.08.108 . **No code or unified data deposit released for the Galatro paper itself.**
- **Reported headline metric:** Pack-level relative error **1–12 %** on capacity fade / time-to-failure (abstract). Tesla-S–like 7104-cell simulation: average **6.1 %** time-to-failure error (range 0.7–17.6 %); **4.0 %** capacity-fade error (range 0.9–11.0 %). At 20-cell scale, time-to-failure errors at low fade thresholds (~10 %) reach ~15–20 % — paper notes NHGP is weakest in early life. Cell sampling: SSPE drops below 10 % when sampling ~25 cells out of 80 in parallel (cost-benefit "sweet spot").
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**
  - **Baumhöfer et al. 2014** (paper's own dataset) — 14 of 51 cells, 18650 LCO/NMC — match: **direct (paper uses it)** — access: medium (extract from JPS Fig. 3 / supplementary) — https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jpowsour.2013.08.108
  - **NASA PCoE Battery Aging dataset** — 18650 LCO cells, randomised usage profiles — match: close (similar form factor, broader operating envelope) — access: easy (NASA Open Data Portal) — https://www.nasa.gov/intelligent-systems-division/discovery-and-systems-health/pcoe/pcoe-data-set-repository/
  - **CALCE Battery Group** (U Maryland) — multiple chemistries, cycling + calendar — match: close — access: easy — https://calce.umd.edu/battery-data
  - **Severson et al. 2019** (MIT/Stanford) — 124 LFP fast-charging cells, well-curated and large-by-LIB-standards — match: loose (LFP not LCO/NMC; cycling-only fast-charge focus) but **the cleanest large public Li-ion dataset for ML benchmarking** — access: easy — https://data.matr.io/1/
  - **Battery Archive** aggregator — multiple chemistries unified under one schema — match: close — access: easy — https://www.batteryarchive.org/
- **Data access difficulty (for the paper's own dataset):** **Medium** — Baumhöfer 2014 raw values must be extracted from Fig. 3 / supplementary of the source JPS paper. NASA / CALCE / Severson alternatives are easier and would replace the original dataset for an ML reproduction.
- **Tooling I would need to learn:** Standard scientific-Python stack — NumPy, SciPy (`scipy.stats.gamma`, `scipy.optimize` for MLE), scikit-learn, optional GPyTorch / Pyro for probabilistic deep regressor, optional PyMC for Bayesian fitting. Monte Carlo for pack rollup is trivial NumPy. **No domain-specific ML libraries** (no RDKit, no GNNs, no pymatgen). Same tooling-friendly profile as Article 1.
- **Portfolio angle — what a good GitHub project based on this would look like:** A clean three-notebook benchmark: (1) **Reproduce Galatro's NHGP** on the Baumhöfer 2014 cell-level data (or a NASA/CALCE substitute), recovering per-cell `(a, b, c)` Gamma parameters and the 1–12 % time-to-failure error band. (2) **Replace the NHGP with a learned probabilistic model** — deep ensemble or Bayesian NN over `(time, T, SOC, ...)` → capacity fade — and compare calibration / coverage to the NHGP baseline. (3) **Reproduce the pack-level Monte Carlo rollup** (Tesla-S 74p6s × 16 architecture) using both the NHGP and the ML model, and compare time-to-failure distributions. The README's headline claim: *"A learned probabilistic model matches Galatro's NHGP on per-cell calibration and matches the 6.1 % pack-level time-to-failure error on a simulated Tesla-S architecture, while removing the closed-form Gamma assumption."* Distinct from Article 1's portfolio angle because **probabilistic forecasting + pack-level Monte Carlo rollup** is the new piece — Article 1 was point regression on stress factors; Article 2 adds the uncertainty-quantification and pack-rollup dimensions that battery management systems actually need.

Pairs naturally with Article 1 as a two-notebook portfolio: Article 1 = "why averaged-α regression breaks", Article 2 = "stochastic-process replacement that fixes it" — with the ML student replacing the parametric bits with learned models in both.

### Article 3: Battery Health Diagnosis Approach Integrating Physics-based Modeling with Electrochemical Impedance Spectroscopy

- **Full citation:** **Galatro, D.**, Da Silva, C., Romero, D. A., Gong, Z., Trescases, O., & Amon, C. H. (2022). Battery Health Diagnosis Approach Integrating Physics‐Based Modeling with Electrochemical Impedance Spectroscopy. *Energy Technology*, 10(4), 2100942. DOI: 10.1002/ente.202100942
- **Authorship caveat:** Galatro is again **first author and sole corresponding author**. Same lab cluster as Articles 1 and 2 (Da Silva, Romero, Trescases, Amon) plus Z. Gong (ECE, online-EIS hardware co-author from her cited APEC 2018 paper). **Third consecutive first-author flagship paper — the strongest lab-signal pattern in the survey so far.**
- **Problem in plain English:** Existing battery-health diagnosis methods can't cleanly tell apart the different physical mechanisms that age a Li-ion cell. Incremental Capacity / Differential Voltage (IC-DV) tests are slow (~10 h per cell) and can't isolate per-electrode contributions; Electrochemical Impedance Spectroscopy (EIS) alone collapses Solid-Electrolyte-Interface (SEI) growth and Lithium Plating (LP) into a single resistance term. Galatro proposes **MSPM-EIS**, which couples a *Modified Single Particle Model* (electrochemical performance + lumped thermal + Tafel-kinetics side reactions) with EIS-fitted equivalent-circuit resistances and time-constants to translate impedance data into separated overpotentials — ohmic, SEI, LP, and diffusion-mechanical — and proposes redefining battery first-life / second-life thresholds in terms of the SEI-vs-LP loss-contribution ratio rather than the legacy "EOL = 80% capacity" rule.
- **ML task type:** **No ML in the paper.** The paper is pure physics-based modelling + multi-objective parameter identification via genetic algorithm in MATLAB. The natural ML reframe — and the strongest in the Galatro section — is **regression of Li-ion capacity (or segregated overpotential contributions) from EIS spectra**: a tabular-or-spectral supervised-regression task with an *exact* public benchmark in Zhang et al. 2020 *Nature Communications* (which the Galatro paper itself cites as ref [15]). A natural extension is **multi-class classification of life-stage** (first-life / second-life / unusable) using the SEI/LP overpotential ratio as the supervisory label.
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality (paper): EIS Nyquist spectra (impedance vs. frequency) on the cells, fitted to the Adapted Randles Equivalent Circuit Model (AR-ECM) yielding R_OHM, R_SEI, R_LP, R_ct, Warburg term + corresponding time constants; cell capacity (Ah); voltage discharge curves at 1C / 2C / 5C / 10C; surface temperature traces; OCV(SOC) curves.
  - Modality (ML reframe): Either the **raw EIS spectrum** (~60 frequencies × {Re Z, Im Z} = ~120 features per measurement, as in Zhang 2020) OR the **fitted EC parameters** (R_OHM, R_SEI, R_LP, time constants, ~5–8 features). Optionally augmented with cell temperature and cycle index.
  - Shape/size: For the paper itself, **not stated as a dataset** — implementation references discharge data + EIS aging data taken from Maheshwari, A. (2018) PhD thesis (TU Eindhoven) for one LFP 6 Ah cell + NMC cells across multiple cycling conditions over ~1840 days. For the ML reframe via Zhang 2020, the public dataset is ~20,000 EIS spectra paired with capacity for LCO 18650 cells at 25°C / 35°C / 45°C.
  - Preprocessing in the paper: EIS spectra fitted via complex non-linear least-squares (CNLS) to the AR-ECM; unknown thermal / electrochemical parameters of MSPM estimated by multi-objective genetic algorithm in MATLAB minimising sum-of-squared-error against measured voltage and temperature curves.
- **Output representation:**
  - What the model predicts (paper): Capacity Indicator CI(%), operating voltage V_op (V), cell temperature T_cell (°C), and the four segregated overpotentials η_OHM, η_SEI, η_LP, η_DIFF (V); plus a derived SEI/LP overpotential ratio used to declare first-life / second-life / unusable thresholds.
  - What the model predicts (ML reframe): Either continuous CI(%) regression target, or a multi-class life-stage label (first / second / unusable), or multi-output regression of the four overpotential contributions.
  - Units / label space: Capacity in % of nominal (or Ah); overpotentials in volts; life-stage labels are 3-way categorical (with thresholds the student picks from the paper: first-life ends when SEI contribution drops below 99.5%; second-life ends when ohmic overpotential exhibits a sharp jump and diffusion losses become detectable in the voltage residual).
- **Model architecture (brief):** No ML. **MSPM** = Modified Single Particle Model (Butler-Volmer ionic-flux sub-module + electrolyte-polynomial sub-module extending validity to high C-rates following Li et al. 2017 + lumped thermal sub-module following Makinejad et al. 2015) + Tafel-kinetics side reactions for SEI and LP, coupled via overpotential addition/subtraction to AR-ECM-fitted resistances obtained from EIS. Parameter identification by multi-objective GA in MATLAB.
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: Maheshwari, A. (2018) thesis dataset — LFP 6 Ah cell (used for performance validation at 1C/2C/5C/10C, 25°C ambient) + NMC cells (used for aging validation at 45°C, DOD = 50%, 1C charge / 1C discharge, ~1840 days).
  - Size: Not stated as a unified count. Implementation uses one LFP cell for the performance figures and one NMC cycling condition for the headline aging figure, plus mention of analysed NMC cells across multiple cycling conditions yielding SEI/LP ratios in the 0.3–2.0 range.
  - Source: Maheshwari, A., 2018. *Modelling, aging and optimal operation of lithium-ion batteries.* PhD thesis, Technische Universiteit Eindhoven (TU/e). Available via the TU/e research repository (research.tue.nl) — verify the link before relying on it.
  - Public? **Partial** — the Maheshwari thesis is publicly downloadable from the TU/e repository, but raw cycling / EIS measurement files are typically embedded in thesis figures and supplementary spreadsheets rather than released as a standalone CSV bundle. **No data deposit and no code release for the Galatro paper itself.**
  - Link: TU/e thesis repository (search "Maheshwari 2018 lithium-ion batteries"); not provided directly in the Galatro paper.
- **Reported headline metric:** Performance side — "excellent agreement" between MSPM-predicted and measured operating voltage and cell temperature for the LFP cell across 1C/2C/5C/10C discharge at 25°C ambient (Figs. 3–4 of the paper). Aging side — predicted CI tracks measured CI within **~8%** through 1840 days for the NMC cell at 45°C, DOD = 50%, 1C/1C cycling; the ohmic overpotential jumps **+31.8%** at the unusable-life boundary; SEI/LP ratio at end of second-life is 1.2 for the headline NMC cell and ranges 0.3–2.0 across the analysed conditions; first-life EOL is identified at **78.2% CI** (vs. the industry 80% rule) using the proposed 99.5%-SEI-contribution threshold. **No standardised held-out-test metric is reported — validation is by visual / percentage agreement against the Maheshwari thesis curves.**
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**
  - **Zhang et al. 2020 EIS dataset** (Nature Communications, "Identifying degradation patterns of lithium ion batteries from impedance spectroscopy using machine learning") — ~**20,000 EIS spectra** paired with capacity for LCO 18650 cells at 25 °C / 35 °C / 45 °C — match: **CLOSE** (exact same input/output supervised-regression task; Galatro paper itself cites it as ref [15]) — access: **EASY** — CC-BY data deposit on Zenodo (search "Zhang 2020 lithium-ion EIS Zenodo"; verify the DOI before relying on it).
  - **NASA PCoE Battery Aging Dataset** (Prognostics Center of Excellence, NASA Ames) — multiple 18650 cells with periodic EIS measurements alongside capacity-fade cycling — match: **CLOSE** for the "EIS → capacity" reframe; **LOOSE** for the SEI/LP segregation specifically (no ground-truth segregated labels) — access: **EASY** — direct download from ti.arc.nasa.gov/tech/dash/groups/pcoe/prognostic-data-repository/.
  - **CALCE Battery Group datasets** (University of Maryland, Center for Advanced Life Cycle Engineering) — capacity-fade cycling + impedance at intervals for several LCO / LFP / NMC cells — match: **LOOSE** (less standardised EIS coverage than Zhang 2020) — access: **EASY** — public download with a click-through agreement at calce.umd.edu/battery-data.
  - **Maheshwari, A., 2018 thesis dataset** (TU Eindhoven) — the paper's own underlying dataset — match: **CLOSE** (it *is* the dataset used) — access: **MEDIUM** — thesis PDF is public on the TU/e repository, but raw cycling / EIS values typically need to be extracted from thesis figures / supplementary files rather than loaded from a CSV.
- **Data access difficulty (for the paper's own dataset):** **Medium** — the Maheshwari thesis is publicly available on the TU/e repository, but raw cycling and EIS measurement values usually require extraction from thesis figures or supplementary spreadsheets rather than direct CSV download. **Easier than Articles 1 / 2** if the student substitutes Zhang 2020 (the strongest exact-match public benchmark in the entire Galatro section, also cited by the Galatro paper itself).
- **Tooling I would need to learn:** Standard Python ML stack — pandas, NumPy, scikit-learn, LightGBM / XGBoost (familiar carry-over from Articles 1 and 2), optional PyTorch for a 1D CNN over the impedance spectrum (familiar). **One genuinely new domain library:** **`impedance.py`** (https://impedancepy.readthedocs.io) — a well-documented, easy-to-learn Python package for fitting EIS data to equivalent-circuit models such as Galatro's AR-ECM. `scipy.optimize` works as a fallback if `impedance.py` is awkward. **No GNNs, no RDKit, no pymatgen, no Materials Project API, no MATLAB required.** The same tooling-friendly profile as Articles 1 and 2.
- **Portfolio angle — what a good GitHub project based on this would look like:** Train a **LightGBM regressor (and a 1D-CNN baseline) on the Zhang et al. 2020 Nature Communications EIS dataset** (~20,000 spectra paired with capacity, public on Zenodo) to predict cell Capacity Indicator from EIS alone, then **compare against a simplified physics-only baseline** that you implement using `impedance.py` to fit the AR-ECM and Galatro's overpotential-translation step. README headline: *"ML matches the Galatro 2022 MSPM-EIS pipeline at predicting Li-ion capacity from impedance, with no thermal / electrochemical parameter estimation."* Bonus notebook: re-cast the Galatro SEI/LP-ratio thresholds as **3-way life-stage classification** (first / second / unusable) and report confusion matrix on held-out cells. Bridge to Articles 1 and 2: a single 3-notebook portfolio where Article 1 = "why averaged-α regression breaks", Article 2 = "stochastic-process replacement", Article 3 = "EIS-driven ML SOH prediction" — the strongest unified narrative across any professor in the survey so far for an EV / BMS / battery-health-prediction recruiter audience.

**Article 3 verdict:** **The strongest single article in Galatro's section** — it is the only one of the three with a directly-matched, single-URL-download public benchmark (Zhang 2020 Nature Communications), and the EIS → capacity reframe maps onto a recruiter-legible "ML for battery health" headline that's hotter than either of the parametric-aging framings in Articles 1 / 2. Pairs into a 3-notebook portfolio with the previous two: Article 1 dissects field-standard regression, Article 2 replaces it with a stochastic process, Article 3 swaps the underlying signal from cycling-time tables to EIS spectra and lets the ML do the heavy lifting.

### Summary for this professor

- **Common thread across their work (based on Articles 1, 2, and 3):** Multi-physics modelling of lithium-ion battery aging in EV-relevant regimes, anchored on the same lab cluster (Da Silva, Romero, Trescases, Amon at U of T MIE) with Galatro as first / corresponding author on every paper. Article 1 dissects why the field-standard averaged-α regression (Mathieu et al. 2017) breaks across chemistries and stress regimes; Article 2 proposes a 3-parameter non-homogeneous Gamma process replacement that captures cell-to-cell variation natively and rolls up to module/pack level via Monte Carlo; Article 3 swaps the input signal from accelerated-aging cycling tables to EIS spectra and couples a Modified Single Particle Model with the EIS-fitted equivalent circuit to segregate ohmic, SEI, lithium-plating, and diffusion-mechanical loss contributions. **All three papers contain zero learned ML** — the lab's expertise is statistical / electrochemical reliability modelling rather than ML — but each one ports cleanly to a portfolio ML reframe (parametric regression in Article 1, probabilistic forecasting in Article 2, EIS-spectrum supervised regression in Article 3).
- **Dominant data modalities:** (i) Tabular accelerated-aging measurements (Articles 1 and 2) — small datasets (tens to hundreds of rows), assembled from cited public sources; (ii) **EIS spectra fitted to the Adapted Randles equivalent-circuit model** (Article 3) — ~120-feature impedance vectors per cell-state with capacity as the supervisory target. Article 3 unlocks a substantially larger and easier-to-access public benchmark (Zhang 2020 Nature Communications, ~20,000 spectra) than either of the cycling-time-table sources used in Articles 1 / 2.
- **Biggest obstacle for me to replicate their work publicly:** Data assembly **for Articles 1 and 2** — the underlying cycling-time-table datasets (Keil 2016, Wikner 2017, Baumhöfer 2014, MOBICUS) are public but live in source-paper tables, theses, and supplementary materials rather than as unified downloadable CSVs. **Article 3 is materially easier** thanks to the Zhang 2020 Zenodo deposit and NASA PCoE substitute. **No code release** for any of the three Galatro papers themselves.
- **Skills I would gain by working with them:** Battery aging / RUL / SOH prediction with proper uncertainty quantification (a recognised industrial ML problem); accelerated-test experimental design + sample-size analysis; multi-factor regression with full goodness-of-fit diagnostics; Gamma stochastic processes + Monte Carlo pack rollup; Arrhenius-style acceleration factors as physics-informed inputs; cross-chemistry transfer; pack-level architecture-aware modelling (parallel sum / series min compositions); **EIS spectroscopy + equivalent-circuit fitting (`impedance.py`) + segregating SEI / lithium-plating / diffusion-mechanical loss contributions from impedance** (new with Article 3); reduced-order electrochemical modelling (single-particle models with electrolyte and thermal sub-models) and parameter identification by genetic algorithm.

## Frank Gu

**Affiliation:** University of Toronto (Department of Chemical Engineering and Applied Chemistry, 200 College St, Toronto). **Dual affiliation on Article 1's byline:** University of Waterloo (Department of Chemical Engineering + Waterloo Institute of Nanotechnology) listed first, U of T listed third — consistent with the period during which Frank Gu transitioned his primary appointment from Waterloo to U of T. Corresponding-author email on Article 1 is `f.gu@utoronto.ca`.

**Lab focus (one sentence):** **Inferred** from Article 1 alone (only one paper processed so far): heterogeneous nanoscale photocatalysts for water-treatment chemistry — synthesis of noble-metal-decorated semiconductor nanoparticles (e.g. Ag, Au, Pt, Pd on TiO₂), characterisation by TEM / BET / DLS / EELS, and mechanistic study of photocatalytic redox chemistry for contaminants of concern (selenate, and by extension chromate, nitrate, organic pollutants). **Verify against Frank Gu's broader publication history before treating this as authoritative — the lab almost certainly works on biomedical / drug-delivery nanoparticle topics as well, which would substantially change the modality and dataset analysis.**

**Lab webpage / Scholar:** Not yet provided — needs to be sourced.

### Article 1: Enhanced Photocatalytic Selectivity of Noble Metallized TiO₂ Nanoparticles for the Reduction of Selenate in Water — Tunable Se Reduction Product H₂Se(g) vs. Se(s)

- **Full citation:** Holmes, A. B., Khan, D., de Oliveira Livera, D., & **Gu, F.** (2020). Enhanced photocatalytic selectivity of noble metallized TiO₂ nanoparticles for the reduction of selenate in water: tunable Se reduction product H₂Se(g) vs. Se(s). *Environmental Science: Nano*. DOI: 10.1039/d0en00048e.
- **Authorship caveat:** Frank Gu is **last author and sole corresponding author** — i.e., this is a senior-PI-signature paper, not a middle-authored contribution. First author Holmes is the PhD-student lead. Lab-signal strength is therefore HIGH (comparable to Galatro's first-/corresponding-authored Articles 1–3), although it's only one paper, so the inference about the lab's broader research direction is correspondingly thinner.
- **Problem in plain English:** Selenium contaminates water through mining, oil & gas refining, and coal-power generation; the WHO and U.S. EPA limits are 10 µg L⁻¹ and 1.5 µg L⁻¹ respectively, well above many industrial discharges. The two main soluble Se species — selenate (SeO₄²⁻) and selenite (SeO₃²⁻) — are hard to remove because selenate adsorbs only weakly to mineral surfaces. Photocatalytic reduction over TiO₂ works but the mechanism is messy: Se can end up either as a solid (Se⁰, recoverable as a deposit on the catalyst) or as a toxic gas (H₂Se, which is volatile but in principle capturable for reuse). The paper shows that **decorating TiO₂ with a noble metal whose work function matches a target reduction potential lets you steer the final product**: low-work-function metals (Au, Ag, Pt) form a direct-Z-scheme arrangement that funnels electrons into the Se conduction band and produces H₂Se gas; high-work-function Pd acts purely as an electron sink and traps the reaction at solid Se⁰. The selectivity story is mechanistically grounded in Schottky-junction physics, Fermi-level equilibration, and quantised double-layer charging on metal nanoparticles.
- **ML task type:** **No ML in the paper at all.** The paper is purely experimental wet-chemistry photocatalysis with mechanistic interpretation supported by literature work-function values and standard band-position diagrams. The natural ML reframes for a portfolio project are:
  1. **Tabular regression on catalyst descriptors → Se-product selectivity (%H₂Se vs %Se⁰).** Inputs: noble-metal work function (eV), nanoparticle size (nm), support band-gap edges (eV vs. SHE), electron-hole-scavenger concentration, pH, photon fluence. Output: continuous selectivity. Tiny dataset from this paper alone (4 metals × ~6 fluence points), so the project lives or dies on whether you can extend with literature data via Catalysis Hub or ChemDataExtractor scraping.
  2. **GNN on adsorbate-on-slab DFT structures → adsorption energy → activity descriptor.** This is the Open Catalyst Project (OC20 / OC22) formalism. Selenate is *not* in the OC20 benchmark, but the same-shape task — predict an adsorption energy on a metal/oxide surface — is exactly what OC20 was built for, with 1.3M relaxations of public DFT data.
  3. **TEM-micrograph segmentation / nanoparticle-size estimation** on the kind of images shown in Fig. 1 of this paper. Mature CNN task; multiple public TEM datasets exist.
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality (paper): TEM micrographs of catalysts (Fig. 1 a–e); BET surface area; DLS hydrodynamic diameter; HR-TEM + EELS for Se deposit characterisation (Fig. 3 b–e); concentration time-series of Se(aq), Se(s), H₂Se(g) under fluence, plus hydroperoxyl radical generation under O₂ (Fig. 7); literature work-function values (4.26 / 5.1 / 5.2 / 5.5 eV vs. vacuum for Ag / Au / Pt / Pd respectively).
  - Modality (tabular ML reframe): ~5–10 catalyst descriptors per row (work function, particle size, support, scavenger, pH, fluence, intensity); 4 metals × ~6 fluence points × ~3 measured Se species ≈ ~70 rows from this paper directly.
  - Modality (OC20 / OC22 GNN reframe): per-atom 3D coordinates + element identifiers + per-system metadata (slab termination, adsorbate identity); typically 20–200 atoms per system.
  - Modality (TEM-image reframe): ~100s–1000s of TEM micrographs per published paper; the Article 1 figures alone give ≤10 calibrated images.
  - Shape / size if stated: 4 catalysts at 1 wt% noble-metal loading on Aeroxide P25 TiO₂; ∼10–50 nm TiO₂ particle diameter; 55 m² g⁻¹ BET surface area; noble-metal deposits 2–6 nm; 0.06 mM selenate, 6.5 mM formic acid, 0.2 g L⁻¹ catalyst, pH 3, 300 K, photon irradiance 1.069 × 10¹⁵ photons cm⁻² s⁻¹ at 365 nm.
  - Preprocessing in the paper: deposition–precipitation (Au) and photodeposition (Ag, Pt, Pd) syntheses; calcination at 300 °C; potassium ferrioxalate actinometry to standardise photon fluence; HG-ICP-OES for Se quantification; DHE fluorimetry calibrated against Fremy's-salt control for hydroperoxyl detection.
- **Output representation:**
  - What the model predicts (paper): nothing; the paper reports measured selectivities and rates rather than predicted ones.
  - What the model would predict (ML reframe): For the tabular reframe, S(H₂Se) ∈ [0, 1] and S(Se⁰) ∈ [0, 1] (a 2-component compositional output, naturally a Dirichlet-regression or softmax target). For the OC20 reframe, an adsorption energy in eV per surface-adsorbate pair. For the TEM reframe, a per-pixel binary mask of nanoparticle / background plus a centroid + size measurement.
  - Units / label space: Selectivities in %; rate constants in mol cm² L⁻¹ × 10⁻²⁴ photons (paper's reported zeroth-order rate is k_Se,0 = 6.210 in those units); adsorption energies in eV; particle sizes in nm.
- **Model architecture (brief):** No ML in the paper. For the portfolio reframe: LightGBM or random-forest regressor for the tabular angle; SchNet / GemNet-OC / EquiformerV2 for the OC20-style GNN angle; U-Net or Mask R-CNN for the TEM-segmentation angle.
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: Internal experimental dataset generated by the Holmes / Gu group at Waterloo / U of T.
  - Size: 4 catalysts (Au-, Ag-, Pt-, Pd–TiO₂) + 1 control (unmodified TiO₂); ~6–8 fluence points per catalyst per Se species; selectivity profiles in Fig. 6; hydroperoxyl-generation linear-fit data in Fig. 7. **Not stated as a unified row count.**
  - Source: Generated in the authors' lab.
  - Public? **No** — the ESI is figures only (Figs. S1–S4), with no consolidated CSV deposit and no code.
  - Link: Not provided in the paper.
- **Reported headline metric:** Selectivity rather than a held-out-test ML metric. **Au–TiO₂ produces ~100% H₂Se selectivity at 2.5 × 10¹⁹ photons cm⁻² (Fig. 6c)**; **Pd–TiO₂ produces ~40% solid Se⁰ at peak (Fig. 5c) and substantially suppresses H₂Se generation**; selenate-removal rate scales inversely with metal work function (Ag fastest → Pd slowest, Fig. 5a). Ranking the four catalysts by H₂Se selectivity at 2.5 × 10¹⁹ photons cm⁻²: Au > Ag ≈ Pt > unmodified TiO₂ > Pd.
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**
  - **Open Catalyst Project — OC20 / OC22** (Meta AI / CMU / FAIR-Chem) — ~**1.3 M DFT relaxations**, ~**260 M atomic structures**, with predefined train / val / test splits — match: **ANALOGY** (selenate reduction is not in the benchmark, but the formalism — predict adsorption energy on a metal-on-oxide surface from atomic structure — is exactly the same task class) — access: **EASY** — direct download from opencatalystproject.org, MIT-licensed, with FAIR-Chem providing pretrained GNN baselines (SchNet, GemNet-OC, EquiformerV2).
  - **Catalysis Hub** (catalysis-hub.org, DTU) — public DFT + experimental catalysis database, including reaction energies and surface scans across many metal / oxide systems — match: **LOOSE** for selenate specifically; **CLOSE** for the general "predict catalytic property from descriptors" task — access: **EASY** — REST API + Python client.
  - **Materials Project** (materialsproject.org, LBNL) — band-gap, work-function, and surface-energy data for transition-metal oxides and noble metals — match: **LOOSE** (gives you the *features* the paper uses to rationalise its mechanism: band edges, work functions, support band gap; you'd still need experimental selectivity labels from another source) — access: **EASY** — `mp-api` Python client, free account required (already used by Sánchez-Lengeling Article 2 and Moosavi Articles 1–3 in this survey).
  - **Open TEM-image datasets** (e.g., DeepEM, NanoMine, the NIST-AFM/TEM repository) — match: **CLOSE** for the nanoparticle-segmentation reframe; **NONE** for the photocatalysis chemistry — access: **EASY** for most, with CC-BY licensing.
  - **Literature-mining via ChemDataExtractor / Catalysis Hub scraping of photocatalysis papers** — match: **CLOSE** if you can extract selectivity / rate data from related Tan et al., Nguyen et al., Sanuki et al., and Kikuchi & Sakamoto papers cited in this article — access: **HARD** (requires NER + table-extraction effort comparable to the Edwards Article 1 PaCE-style assembly).
- **Data access difficulty (for the paper's own dataset):** **Hard** — the paper releases no consolidated CSV, no code, and the ESI is figures-only. The only practical paths to data for a portfolio project are (i) digitise the ~4–5 selectivity / rate plots from Figs. 4–7 by hand (small dataset, easy but limited) or (ii) substitute Open Catalyst Project / Catalysis Hub for the catalyst-screening reframe, accepting that the reaction is not selenate.
- **Tooling I would need to learn:** **Heaviest new-tooling lift in the survey so far for any chemistry / materials article.** Standard carry-over: pandas, NumPy, scikit-learn, PyTorch, matplotlib. **New for the OC20-style reframe:** `pymatgen` (carry-over from Sánchez-Lengeling and Moosavi sections), `ASE` (Atomic Simulation Environment — new, well-documented), the **FAIR-Chem / OC framework** (new, tightly integrated with `ase` and `torch_geometric`), and **graph neural networks for atomic structures** (SchNet, GemNet-OC, EquiformerV2 — new, the student's CLAUDE.md explicitly flags GNN-on-molecules as a from-scratch tool). **New for the TEM-image reframe:** OpenCV / scikit-image, U-Net or Mask R-CNN in PyTorch (mostly familiar), the `ImageJ` ecosystem for ground-truth annotation (new but standard in materials labs). The pure tabular reframe has the lowest tooling cost (familiar sklearn / LightGBM only).
- **Portfolio angle — what a good GitHub project based on this would look like:** Three viable framings, in increasing order of ambition and recruiter-impact:
  1. **Lightest — tabular descriptors → selectivity regression.** Hand-digitise the selectivity time-series from Figs. 5–6 (the paper's own 4 catalysts × ~6 fluence points), augment with ~10–20 additional NM-TiO₂ photocatalysis data points from the cited Tan / Nguyen / Sanuki / Kikuchi & Sakamoto papers, train a LightGBM regressor with work function + particle size + scavenger + fluence as features. README claim: *"Tabular regression reproduces the qualitative work-function → H₂Se-selectivity ordering reported by Holmes & Gu 2020 from literature descriptors alone."* Realistic and concrete; tooling cost low; portfolio impact moderate.
  2. **Medium — TEM-image segmentation for nanoparticle sizing.** Train a U-Net on a public TEM-micrograph dataset to detect and size noble-metal nanoparticles, then apply it to the Holmes & Gu Fig. 1 / Fig. 3 images and reproduce the BET / DLS / particle-size table (Table 1) from images alone. README claim: *"End-to-end CNN reproduces noble-metal-deposit size distributions from TEM micrographs without manual ImageJ annotation."* Higher portfolio impact than (1); tooling lift moderate.
  3. **Heaviest — Open Catalyst Project GNN on a related reaction.** Train (or fine-tune from a FAIR-Chem checkpoint) an EquiformerV2 model on an OC20 sub-task (e.g. CO₂ reduction or H₂ evolution on noble-metal-on-TiO₂ surfaces) and use the predicted adsorption-energy descriptors to rank the four catalysts in Holmes & Gu. README claim: *"GNN-predicted adsorption-energy descriptors reproduce the experimental selectivity ranking of Holmes & Gu 2020 — work-function-controlled bimodal selectivity recovered from atomic structure alone."* Highest portfolio impact (Open Catalyst Project is the field-standard heterogeneous-catalysis benchmark and recruiter-legible at any AI / materials shop); highest tooling cost (full GNN-on-atoms stack).

**Article 1 verdict:** A credible — but **not exceptional** — GitHub project is achievable from this paper, with the realistic project being tabular literature-descriptor regression (Path 1 above) rather than the recruiter-impact OC20-GNN reframe (Path 3) that requires a substantial new-tooling lift. The paper itself is mechanistically rich and reads cleanly, but the ML-portfolio fit is constrained by (i) no released dataset, (ii) no public benchmark for selenate photocatalysis, and (iii) a heavy tooling jump if the student wants the recruiter-legible OC20 path. **Frank Gu enters the survey at rank 7** based on this single article — but the senior-PI / corresponding-author authorship signal is strong, so additional articles from the lab (especially anything closer to the lab's biomedical / drug-delivery side, if that exists) may pull the rollup substantially in either direction.

### Article 2: Influence of Pluronic Coating Formulation on Iron Oxide Nanoparticle Transport in Natural and Oil-Impacted Sandy Aquifer Media

- **Full citation:** Linley, S., Thomson, N. R., McVey, K., Sra, K., & **Gu, F. X.** (2020). Influence of Pluronic coating formulation on iron oxide nanoparticle transport in natural and oil-impacted sandy aquifer media. *The Canadian Journal of Chemical Engineering*, 98(3), 642–649. DOI: 10.1002/cjce.23650 (received 2019-06-04; published online 2020).
- **Authorship caveat:** Frank Gu is **last author but NOT corresponding author** — corresponding author is first-author Stuart Linley (Waterloo PhD student). On-site experimental PI is Neil R. Thomson (Waterloo, Civil & Environmental Engineering); McVey & Sra are industry co-authors from Chevron Energy Technology Company; Gu provides the nanoparticle-synthesis methodology that ties the project to his prior Pluronic / SPION work. **Lab-signal strength is therefore moderate, not strong** — Gu is the senior co-author providing nanoparticle expertise to a Waterloo-led civil-engineering / Chevron-funded soil-remediation project, rather than running the project himself. This is a meaningfully weaker advisor signal than Article 1's last-AND-corresponding role.
- **Problem in plain English:** Soils contaminated with light-non-aqueous-phase-liquid (LNAPL) hydrocarbons such as crude oil are environmentally and economically painful to remediate; conventional approaches dig the soil up. *In situ* remediation by injecting engineered nanoparticles is more attractive, but vanilla "blanket" injection wastes nanoparticles in already-clean zones. Borrowing a concept from oncology nanomedicine — drug-loaded nanocarriers functionalised to bind specifically to tumour-cell receptors — the paper coats iron-oxide (magnetite, ~8 nm) nanoparticles with **Pluronic block copolymers** that have a hydrophilic poly(ethylene oxide) (PEO) tail (gives aqueous stability) plus a hydrophobic poly(propylene oxide) (PPO) middle block (binds the crude oil at the LNAPL/water interface). The experimental question is: how do the Pluronic's PPO/PEO molecular structure and coating concentration trade off (a) transport through saturated sandy-aquifer columns against (b) preferential retention on oil-impacted sand? They run column flow-through experiments on Borden Sand from CFB Borden, Ontario, vs the same sand pre-coated with crude oil at 0.015 g g⁻¹.
- **ML task type:** **No ML in the paper.** The paper is purely experimental column hydrology + nanomaterials characterisation. The natural ML reframes for a portfolio project are unusually clean for this professor:
  1. **Tabular regression on Pluronic / coating descriptors → nanoparticle recovery (%).** Inputs: total polymer MW, MW_PPO, MW_PEO, hydrophilic/hydrophobic block-number ratio, coating concentration (g L⁻¹), soil type (clean vs oily Borden sand), salt environment (Na⁺ / K⁺ / Mg²⁺ / Ca²⁺ / SO₄²⁻ from the salt-stability tests in Figs. S1–S2). Output: continuous recovery in %, optionally augmented to *full breakthrough-curve regression* (concentration vs elution volume). **The paper itself releases the ground-truth values directly in Table 1 + Figs. 2 / 4 / 5 / 6 — yielding ~30–50 hand-digitisable rows from this paper alone, the most digestible internal dataset of any Frank Gu article processed so far.**
  2. **Cross-over to public nanomedicine active-targeting datasets** (Wilhelm 2016 *Nature Reviews Materials* tumour-delivery-efficiency dataset, ~232 datapoints; Lin et al. 2022 *ACS Nano* extension to ~1000 datapoints) — same active-targeting-with-amphiphilic-coating motif (PEG-coated nanocarriers targeting tumours = Pluronic-coated SPIONs targeting oil), and the paper itself draws this analogy explicitly in §1.
  3. **TEM-micrograph CNN classification** distinguishing individually-stabilised SPIONs vs aggregates as a function of coating concentration (Fig. 3).
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality (paper): TEM micrographs (Figs. 1, 3); DLS hydrodynamic-diameter measurements; column-effluent ICP-AES iron-concentration time-series; tabulated Pluronic structural descriptors (Table 1).
  - Modality (tabular ML reframe): ~6–10 features per row — total Pluronic MW, MW_PPO, MW_PEO, hydrophilic/hydrophobic ratio, coating concentration, soil type (binary), and optional salt-environment factors.
  - Modality (Wilhelm/Lin nanomedicine cross-over): ~25 features per row — particle size, zeta potential, shape, core composition, surface modification, targeting strategy, cancer model, dose, etc.
  - Modality (TEM-image reframe): 4 image-classes × ~tens of micrographs per class from Article 1's and Article 2's combined figure set.
  - Shape / size if stated (paper): 7 Pluronics tested in transport (Table 1: L62, P123, P103, P104, P105, F127, F108); SPION cores 8.08 ± 0.76 nm by TEM, hydrodynamic diameters 25–50 nm by DLS at 10 g L⁻¹ Pluronic; columns 15 cm long × 3.8 cm I.D., porosity 0.36 (clean) / 0.38 (oily); injection at 18 cm³ h⁻¹; recoveries reported in triplicate.
  - Preprocessing in the paper: co-precipitation synthesis of magnetite from Fe²⁺/Fe³⁺ in 4 M NH₄OH with oleic-acid capping; phase-transfer to aqueous Pluronic suspension via probe sonication + 48-h separatory-funnel separation; ICP-AES with HCl acid digestion to MDL = 4 µg L⁻¹.
- **Output representation:**
  - What the model predicts (paper): nothing; the paper reports measured recoveries (61.1 ± 1.0% for F108 → 97.4 ± 7.2% for P103 → 13.6% in oily sand at 1 g L⁻¹ → 59.4% in oily sand at 2.5 g L⁻¹) rather than predicted ones.
  - What the model would predict (ML reframe): scalar recovery in %, OR full breakthrough-curve concentration C/C₀ vs elution volume (mL), OR pixel-wise individual/aggregate classification on TEM images.
  - Units / label space: recovery in % ∈ [0, 100]; deposition in mg per kg of sand; particle size in nm; coating concentration in g L⁻¹.
- **Model architecture (brief):** No ML in the paper. Portfolio reframes: LightGBM or Gaussian-process regressor for the tabular angle (small dataset → GP is principled; LightGBM is the recruiter-default); 1D-CNN or transformer-encoder for the BTC-curve angle; U-Net or ResNet classifier for the TEM angle.
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: Internal experimental dataset generated by Linley & Thomson at Waterloo CEE / Waterloo Institute for Nanotechnology, with Pluronic samples gifted by BASF Canada and crude-oil samples + funding from Chevron Energy Technology Company.
  - Size: 7 Pluronics × 3 column replicates each = 21 BTCs in Fig. 2; 3 coating concentrations × 3 column replicates = 9 BTCs in Fig. 4; 2 sand-type × 2 coating concentrations × 2–3 column replicates = ~10 BTCs in Figs. 5–6; salt-stability tests at 0–3 mol L⁻¹ across 5 cation/anion species (Figs. S1–S2). **~30–50 directly readable rows total from the figures + Table 1.**
  - Source: Generated in the authors' labs.
  - Public? **No** — the supplementary information adds Tables S1 (TEM size statistics) and S2 (per-replicate recovery percentages), but no consolidated CSV or code is released.
  - Link: Not provided in the paper. The supplementary information is on the publisher's web page (Wiley Online Library).
- **Reported headline metric:** F108 (longest PEO chains, MW = 15 000 g/mol) gives **61.1 ± 1.0% recovery** through clean Borden sand; P103 (shortest PEO chains at the 3000 g/mol PPO segment) gives **97.4 ± 7.2% recovery**; recovery scales monotonically inversely with PEO-chain length and total Pluronic hydrophilicity. The 4:1 L62:P104 blend at 1 g L⁻¹ shows preferential oil retention: 29.2% recovery in clean Borden sand vs 13.6% in oily Borden sand — i.e., **2.1× higher retention on the oil-contaminated sand at the operationally-optimal coating formulation**.
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**
  - **Wilhelm et al. 2016 *Nature Reviews Materials* nanomedicine delivery-efficiency dataset** — ~117 papers, ~232 datapoints, nanoparticle physicochemistry (size, zeta, shape, core, coating, targeting strategy, cancer model) → tumour delivery efficiency (% of injected dose) — match: **CLOSE ANALOGY** (the paper itself draws this exact analogy in §1; same active-targeting-with-amphiphilic-coating framework; just oil-targeted instead of tumour-targeted) — access: **EASY** — the original dataset is in the paper's supplementary information; later extensions exist.
  - **Lin et al. 2022 *ACS Nano* nanocarrier delivery extension** — extends the Wilhelm dataset to ~1000 datapoints with newer nanomedicine literature — match: **CLOSE ANALOGY** (same as Wilhelm but ~5× larger) — access: **EASY** — public CSV deposit.
  - **NanoMine / NanoCommons** open material-property databases for engineered nanomaterials (including iron-oxide nanoparticles + their surface modifications + measured biological / environmental endpoints) — match: **LOOSE** for soil-transport specifically, **CLOSE** for the general "nanoparticle physicochemistry → endpoint" task — access: **EASY** — REST API + Python clients.
  - **Hand-digitisation of this paper's own Figs. 2 / 4 / 5 / 6 + Table 1** — yields ~30–50 rows of (Pluronic descriptor, soil type, recovery%) that map directly onto a tabular regression target — match: **EXACT** but tiny — access: **EASY** but manual.
  - **Open TEM-image datasets** (DeepEM, NanoMine TEM) for the segmentation reframe — match: **CLOSE** for the image task; **NONE** for the soil-remediation chemistry — access: **EASY**, CC-BY licensing.
- **Data access difficulty (for the paper's own dataset):** **Medium** — no consolidated CSV but Table 1 + Figs. 2 / 4 / 5 / 6 are directly digestible by hand to ~30–50 rows. Substantially easier than Article 1's figures-only ESI thanks to Table 1's explicit tabular Pluronic descriptors.
- **Tooling I would need to learn:** **Substantially lighter than Article 1's heaviest path.** Standard carry-over: pandas, NumPy, scikit-learn, LightGBM, PyTorch, matplotlib. **Optional new pieces:** Gaussian-process regression (`sklearn.gaussian_process` or `GPyTorch`) for principled small-data uncertainty; `WebPlotDigitizer` (free web tool, not even a Python library) for hand-digitising the BTC plots. **No `ase`, no FAIR-Chem, no GNN-on-atoms required.** This is the most tooling-friendly Frank Gu reframe.
- **Portfolio angle — what a good GitHub project based on this would look like:** Two viable framings:
  1. **Lightest — tabular Pluronic-descriptor regression with public-dataset cross-over.** Hand-digitise this paper's Table 1 + Figs. 2 / 4 / 5 / 6 (~50 rows), then concatenate with the Wilhelm 2016 / Lin 2022 nanomedicine dataset (~1000 rows after subsetting to PEG-PPO-PEG amphiphilic-coated SPION/IONP entries) to get a unified "amphiphilic-block-copolymer-coated nanoparticle → active-targeting recovery%" tabular regression. Train a Gaussian process (small data → calibrated uncertainty) and a LightGBM benchmark. README claim: *"Tabular regression on Pluronic block-copolymer descriptors reproduces the recovery ranking reported by Linley & Gu 2020 with calibrated uncertainty, demonstrating that nanomedicine active-targeting datasets transfer to soil remediation under the framework laid out in §1 of the paper."* This is the **strongest single-paper portfolio framing of any Frank Gu article processed so far** because the cross-over to a public benchmark is *explicitly motivated by the paper itself*, not a stretch the student has to defend.
  2. **Stretch — TEM CNN classifier for individual-vs-aggregate SPION morphology** (Fig. 3 of this paper + Fig. 1 of Article 1). Smaller portfolio impact, identical tooling cost; useful as a side-notebook only.

**Article 2 verdict:** **The strongest single Frank Gu article processed so far** — the tabular Pluronic-descriptor reframe is clean, the nanomedicine-dataset cross-over is explicitly motivated by the paper itself in §1, and the new-tooling cost is minimal compared to Article 1's OC20 path. The authorship caveat (Gu is last but NOT corresponding) means this is a *secondary* lab signal compared to Article 1, but the ML-portfolio fit is meaningfully better. Pairs cleanly with Article 1 as a 2-notebook portfolio: Article 1 = "OC20-style GNN catalyst screening" (heavy), Article 2 = "nanomedicine-cross-over Pluronic regression" (light) — different modalities, different audiences.

### Article 3: Emerging Nanomaterials for the Application of Selenium Removal for Wastewater Treatment (Critical Review)

- **Full citation:** Holmes, A. B., & **Gu, F. X.** (2016). Emerging nanomaterials for the application of selenium removal for wastewater treatment. *Environmental Science: Nano*, 3, 982–996. DOI: 10.1039/c6en00144k.
- **Article type:** **Critical review** — not primary research. Two-author piece (single PhD student + single PI).
- **Authorship caveat:** Frank Gu is **last author and sole corresponding author** (`frank.gu@uwaterloo.ca`); first author Andrew Holmes is the same PhD student who first-authored Article 1's 2020 experimental selenate-photocatalysis paper. **Affiliation on this 2016 byline lists U Waterloo Chem Eng + Waterloo Institute for Nanotechnology only — NO University of Toronto affiliation**, predating the joint-affiliation transition seen on Articles 1 (2020) and 2 (2020). This is a Waterloo-era PI-signature paper, not a U of T-era one.
- **Lab-signal observation worth flagging up front:** This 2016 review is the **direct literature-foundation paper for Article 1's 2020 experimental selenate-photocatalysis study** — same PhD student (Holmes), same PI (Gu), same topic (selenium nano-remediation), and §4.3.2 of this review explicitly describes the TiO₂-photoreduction mechanism that Article 1 then probes experimentally with noble-metal modifications (citing Tan et al. 2003 = ref [71/72/73] of this review = also cited by Article 1). **Articles 1 and 3 form a tightly-coupled review-then-experiment pair on the same topic, by the same trainee, supervised by the same PI** — strongest within-section narrative arc in any professor's section so far.
- **Problem in plain English:** Selenium oxyanions (selenate SeO₄²⁻ and selenite SeO₃²⁻) contaminate industrial wastewater (coal mining, metal smelting, oil refining, agriculture), and the U.S. EPA's 2015 draft rule tightened the aquatic-life chronic limit to 1.2 µg L⁻¹ (lentic) / 3.1 µg L⁻¹ (lotic) — well below what conventional wastewater treatment can deliver. The review surveys the four classes of conventional Se-removal technology (physical, adsorptive, oxidation/reduction, biological — Section 2 + Table 1) along with their gaps (Section 3), then critically reviews **seven families of emerging nanomaterials** for the same task: ligand-immobilised nanocomposites (LINs), nanocrystalline metal oxides (Fe / Ti / Al / Mn-Fe), nanoscale zero-valent iron (nZVI), graphene-oxide composites, layered double hydroxides (LDHs), and polymer-clay nanocomposites — with explicit comparison of selectivity, recyclability, scale-up viability, and adsorption capacities (Q_m in mg g⁻¹) compiled in Table 2.
- **ML task type:** **No ML in the paper** (it's a review). The most ML-portfolio-relevant artifact is **Table 2 itself** — a literature-curated tabular dataset of ~17 nano-adsorbent entries across 7 families with Se species (IV/VI), initial Se concentration (mg L⁻¹), adsorbent concentration (g L⁻¹), pH, temperature (K), and adsorption capacity Q_m (mg g⁻¹). Natural ML reframes:
  1. **Tabular regression on Table 2 → Q_m** (mg g⁻¹). Inputs: nano-adsorbent class (categorical, 7 families), Se species (binary IV/VI), initial conc, adsorbent conc, pH, T. Output: continuous adsorption capacity. ~17 rows from this paper alone, expandable to 50+ by digging into the cited primary papers (each row in Table 2 has a citation → primary-paper extraction).
  2. **Literature-mining / NER pipeline on this review's 99 cited references** to expand Table 2 — frame as a "ChemDataExtractor + LLM for adsorption-capacity literature mining" project. Recruiter-legible angle: combines NLP/IE + chem-eng domain knowledge.
  3. **Cross-section bridge**: combine Table 2 from this review with the experimental selectivity data from Article 1 (Holmes & Gu 2020 selenate photocatalysis) into a unified "ML for selenium nano-remediation" portfolio — the strongest cross-article narrative for any single professor in the survey.
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality (paper): purely curated literature data + schematics; no raw experimental input modality.
  - Modality (ML reframe): tabular ~6-feature row per nano-adsorbent. Categorical features (adsorbent family, Se species), numerical features (initial Se conc, adsorbent conc, pH, T).
  - Shape / size (paper): Table 1 = ~10 conventional-technology comparison rows; **Table 2 = ~17 nano-adsorbent rows** with Q_m labels; Figs. 1 (Pourbaix Se), 2 (BioSeNPs schematic), 3 (SeO₄²⁻ surface complexes), 4 (TiO₂ photoreduction schematic), 5 (nZVI core-shell schematic), 6 (LDH structure).
  - Preprocessing in the paper: none — it's a review.
- **Output representation:**
  - What the model would predict (ML reframe): scalar Q_m (mg g⁻¹), or a multi-output (Q_m for SeIV, Q_m for SeVI), or a binary "below 5 µg L⁻¹ U.S. EPA limit achievable / not achievable" classification target.
  - Units / label space: Q_m in mg g⁻¹ ∈ ~[0.05, 178] across the 17 entries in Table 2.
- **Model architecture (brief):** No ML in the paper. Portfolio reframes: Gaussian-process regressor (small data → calibrated uncertainty is the principled choice for ~17–50 rows); LightGBM or random forest as the recruiter-default benchmark; Bayesian linear regression as a sanity check.
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: Curated literature data compiled from 99 cited primary references (refs [51, 52, 53, 57, 58, 60, 63–65, 67, 75, 76, 77, 78, 79, 88, 91, 96–99] feed Table 2 directly).
  - Size: ~17 rows in Table 2; ~10 rows in Table 1; full set expandable to ~50+ via primary-paper extraction.
  - Source: Compiled by Holmes & Gu from cited primary literature.
  - Public? **Tables themselves are public in the published PDF; no separate data deposit and no code release.**
  - Link: Not provided as a CSV; the review is the artifact.
- **Reported headline metric:** Reviews don't report a single metric. Headline numerical findings: best Se reductive capacity by nZVI = 1.3 mM selenite removed in 3 minutes (ref [56]); LDH max Q_m = 178 mg g⁻¹ for selenite at pH 9 (ref [96], highest in Table 2); ligand-immobilised nanocomposites max Q_m = 111.12 mg g⁻¹ for SeIV at pH 2.5 (ref [58]); MGO graphene-oxide composite >99.9% selenite removal across 10 sorption-elution cycles (ref [65]).
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**
  - **Hand-digitisation of Table 2 itself** (~17 rows) — match: **EXACT** (it *is* the dataset for the paper-internal reframe) — access: **EASY** (one PDF table).
  - **Primary-paper extraction across the ~22 refs that feed Table 2** — extends the dataset to ~50–100 rows — match: **CLOSE** — access: **MEDIUM** (each primary paper requires its own extraction, mostly behind paywalls but indexed by DOI).
  - **NanoMine / NanoCommons** open material-property databases for engineered nanomaterials with measured environmental endpoints — match: **LOOSE** (broader nanomaterial domain than just Se adsorption) — access: **EASY** — REST API + Python clients.
  - **Sun et al. 2020 *Environmental Science: Water Research & Technology* "ML for water treatment" literature-mined adsorption-capacity datasets** for heavy metals — match: **CLOSE ANALOGY** (same task class — predict adsorption capacity from adsorbent + sorbate + conditions; just non-Se sorbates) — access: **EASY** — public dataset deposits.
  - **Direct cross-link with Article 1** (Holmes & Gu 2020 selenate photocatalysis) — match: **EXACT for the photocatalysis subset** — access: **EASY** (already in the survey).
- **Data access difficulty (for the paper's own dataset):** **Easy for the paper-internal Table-2 reframe** (one PDF table) — substantially easier than Articles 1 and 2. **Medium for the extended-primary-paper reframe** (each row requires DOI lookup + figure / table extraction).
- **Tooling I would need to learn:** **Lightest-tooling Frank Gu reframe.** Pure pandas / sklearn / LightGBM / Gaussian-process regression. **No images, no GNNs, no impedance / equivalent-circuit fitting.** Optional: ChemDataExtractor or an LLM-based extraction pipeline if the student pursues the literature-mining angle (Reframe 2). Same tooling profile as Article 2 with one less library (no `WebPlotDigitizer` needed since Table 2 is already tabular).
- **Portfolio angle — what a good GitHub project based on this would look like:** Two viable framings:
  1. **Lightest — tabular regression on Table 2 + a cross-link notebook.** Hand-transcribe Table 2 (~17 rows) into a CSV, train a Gaussian process and a LightGBM benchmark to predict Q_m from (adsorbent family, Se species, conc, pH, T), and produce a calibrated-uncertainty figure showing where in the input space the model is confident vs guessing. README claim: *"Reproducible ML benchmark for selenium nano-adsorption built on Holmes & Gu 2016, with calibrated uncertainty across 7 nanomaterial families."*
  2. **Stretch — literature-mining extension to ~100+ rows + cross-article bridge.** Use ChemDataExtractor or an LLM-based table-extraction pipeline to pull adsorption-capacity rows from the ~22 primary papers cited in Table 2; concatenate with Article 1's experimental selectivity data; train the same models on the larger dataset. README claim: *"End-to-end pipeline from PDF literature mining to ML-predicted Se adsorption capacity, validated against the Holmes & Gu 2016 review and the Holmes & Gu 2020 photocatalysis follow-up."* This is **the most narratively coherent portfolio angle in any professor's section** because it ties two of the same lab's papers together by the same PhD student.
