## Radhakrishnan Mahadevan

**Affiliation:** University of Toronto (Department of Chemical Engineering and Applied Chemistry + Institute of Biomaterials and Biomedical Engineering, joint appointment confirmed by Article 1's byline). One of the long-tenured U of T faculty in the metabolic-engineering / systems-biology space and a recognized name in the constraint-based-modeling community (openCOBRA consortium signatory).

**Lab focus (one sentence):** **Inferred** from Article 1 alone (only one paper processed so far): genome-scale metabolic modeling, constraint-based analysis (flux-balance analysis and its extensions), strain design for metabolic engineering, and the broader systems-biology toolchain (cobrapy, SBML3FBC, MEMOTE) used to engineer microbes for chemical / biofuel / biopharmaceutical production. **One-paper inference; treat as tentative until at least one Mahadevan-first-or-corresponding-author paper is processed — Article 1 below is a multi-author community-tool paper, not Mahadevan-led research.**

**Lab webpage / Scholar:** Not yet provided — needs to be sourced. (Mahadevan is a well-established faculty with a public Scholar profile; verify before finalising.)

### Article 1: MEMOTE for Standardized Genome-Scale Metabolic Model Testing (openCOBRA-Consortium Community Paper)

- **Full citation:** Lieven, C., Beber, M. E., Olivier, B. G., ... **Mahadevan, R.** ... (≈60 co-authors total). (2020). MEMOTE for standardized genome-scale metabolic model testing. *Nature Biotechnology*, 38(3), 271–278. DOI: 10.1038/s41587-020-0446-y. **Article type:** Correspondence in *Nature Biotechnology* — short-format community-consensus paper, not full research article.
- **Authorship caveat — important:** This is a **~60-author openCOBRA-consortium community paper**. Co-first authors are Christian Lieven and Moritz E. Beber (DTU Biosustain). Corresponding author is **Nikolaus Sonnenschein** (DTU Biosustain). **Mahadevan is signatory #22 of the ~60-author list**, listed alphabetically / affiliation-grouped with the other community contributors — **NOT first, last, or corresponding author**. Same authorship-class issue as Sánchez-Lengeling Article 1 and Moosavi Article 1 (high-quality paper with weak PI-specific lab signal). **The 7.3 article-level score reflects portfolio feasibility for a project inspired by this paper, not the strength of Mahadevan's authorship signal.** *Strongly recommend processing at least one Mahadevan-first-or-corresponding-author paper before treating the rollup as a stable advisor signal.*
- **Problem in plain English:** A **genome-scale metabolic model (GEM)** is a large mathematical model of an organism's metabolism — typically thousands of metabolites + thousands of reactions, organised as a stoichiometric matrix S ∈ ℝ^{m×n} plus gene-protein-reaction (GPR) rules and biomass-objective-function definitions — used to predict cellular phenotypes (growth rate, gene knockouts, drug targets, metabolic-engineering interventions) under different environmental conditions. The field has produced 10,000+ published GEMs across multiple software ecosystems (cobrapy, COBRA Toolbox, OptFlux, KBase, etc.) but quality varies wildly: stoichiometric inconsistencies, missing GPR rules, ATP-from-nothing energy-cycle artifacts, and incomplete annotations are common. The paper introduces **MEMOTE** ("metabolic model tests"), an open-source Python test suite that runs ~150 deterministic consensus tests on any SBML3FBC-format GEM across four areas — annotation, basic structural tests, biomass reaction, stoichiometry — and produces a quantitative 0–100 quality score plus a human-readable report. Validates MEMOTE on **10,780 GEMs** drawn from 7 collections (AGORA, CarveMe, Path2Models, KBase, BiGG, Ebrahim et al., OptFlux), showing dramatic across-collection quality variation: ~70% of BiGG models have ≥1 stoichiometrically unbalanced metabolite, up to 50% of Path2Models models are stoichiometrically inconsistent, ~15% of reactions across all collections lack GPR rules.
- **ML task type:** **No supervised ML in the paper.** MEMOTE itself is a rule-based / deterministic test suite — no learned model, no training. The only "ML-adjacent" component is **t-SNE for unsupervised dimensional reduction** of the 10,780-model × ~150-test feature matrix in Fig. 2a. Several natural ML reframes for a portfolio project:
  1. **Tabular regression / classification** on the 10,780-model × ~150-MEMOTE-test feature matrix (publicly available on Zenodo). Predict overall MEMOTE score from a subset of features; identify which tests dominate the score; cluster the 10,780 models by MEMOTE-feature profile and characterise clusters by collection / organism.
  2. **GEM-quality prediction from organism metadata.** Inputs: organism taxonomy (kingdom / phylum / class), genome size (Mb), GC content, number of ORFs, source collection. Output: predicted MEMOTE score before generating the GEM — a "should we trust an automatically-built GEM for this organism?" recommender.
  3. **Graph neural networks on the bipartite metabolite-reaction graph** for gap-filling / missing-reaction prediction. PyTorch Geometric on each model's S-matrix interpreted as a bipartite graph; train a link-predictor on a held-out subset of reactions per model. This is an active research direction in the field (Yang et al. 2019, Kim et al. 2022, etc.).
  4. **Metabolic-engineering target prediction** using cobrapy + MEMOTE-validated GEMs. Predict which gene knockouts / overexpressions yield the highest production of a target metabolite — closer to Mahadevan's actual research direction (strain design / adaptive evolution).
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality (paper): GEMs encoded in SBML3FBC (SBML level 3 with the Flux Balance Constraints package). Each model = stoichiometric matrix S ∈ ℝ^{m×n} + GPR boolean rules per reaction + biomass objective function + reaction reversibility flags + flux bounds + metabolite chemical formulas / charges + cross-database annotations (KEGG, ChEBI, MetaNetX, BiGG IDs, etc.).
  - Modality (ML reframe via tabular MEMOTE outputs): each model produces a ~150-feature vector of test results (binary pass/fail or fractional metrics). 10,780 such vectors are publicly available on Zenodo.
  - Modality (ML reframe via GNN): bipartite graph with metabolite-nodes + reaction-nodes; edges weighted by stoichiometric coefficient. Typical model: ~1,000–3,000 nodes per graph for a bacterial GEM, larger for eukaryotic / human GEMs.
  - Shape / size if stated (paper): 10,780 GEMs total across 7 collections; BiGG = ~80 manually curated models; AGORA = 773 human-gut-microbiota models; CarveMe = ~5,500 bacterial models; KBase + Path2Models substantially larger.
  - Preprocessing in the paper: SBML parsing via `libsbml`; `cobrapy` for constraint-based analysis primitives; MEMOTE applies its ~150 tests; t-SNE on normalised test-result matrix for the 2D visualisation in Fig. 2a.
- **Output representation:**
  - What the model predicts (paper): a per-model MEMOTE score (0–100) plus per-test pass/fail / fractional-metric outcomes; rendered as a human-readable HTML report (snapshot, diff, or history report).
  - What the model would predict (ML reframe): for the tabular reframe, scalar MEMOTE score or per-cluster categorical label; for the GNN reframe, link-prediction probability for held-out reactions; for the metadata reframe, predicted MEMOTE score from organism descriptors.
  - Units / label space: MEMOTE score ∈ [0, 100]; per-test outcomes either binary or fractional ∈ [0, 1].
- **Model architecture (brief):** No ML in the paper. MEMOTE = rule-based Python test suite built on `cobrapy`. Portfolio reframes: LightGBM or random-forest regressor on tabular MEMOTE features (recruiter-default benchmark); GNN (GCN, GAT, or GraphSAGE in PyTorch Geometric) on bipartite metabolite-reaction graphs; sklearn / XGBoost on organism-metadata regression.
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: 7-collection GEM benchmark — AGORA + CarveMe + Path2Models + KBase + BiGG + Ebrahim et al. 2015 + OptFlux models.
  - Size: **10,780 GEMs total**, with per-test result vectors for each.
  - Source: Authors compiled from public collections.
  - Public? **YES — outstanding.** Both the model collection (Zenodo `10.5281/zenodo.2636858`) and the per-model test-result tables + analysis code (Zenodo `10.5281/zenodo.2638234`) are publicly deposited. MEMOTE source code is **Apache-2.0** on GitHub (`opencobra/memote`); web service at `memote.io`; documentation at `memote.readthedocs.io`. **This is the highest-reproducibility paper in the entire survey so far** (matches Sánchez-Lengeling Articles 1 and 2 at perfect-10).
  - Link: https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.2636858 (models) + https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.2638234 (results + analysis code).
- **Reported headline metric:** No single supervised-ML metric (no ML). Headline empirical findings: **~70% of BiGG models** have ≥1 stoichiometrically unbalanced metabolite; **up to 50% of Path2Models models** are stoichiometrically inconsistent; **~15% of reactions across all collections** lack GPR rules; AGORA and KBase contain ~30% blocked reactions, BiGG and OptFlux ~20%, CarveMe and Path2Models very few; only AGORA / KBase / BiGG have SBML-compliant metabolite + reaction annotations; only KBase + selected BiGG models include gene annotations.
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**
  - **The MEMOTE Zenodo deposit itself** — 10,780 models + per-model ~150-feature MEMOTE result vectors — match: **EXACT** (it *is* the dataset for any tabular-feature ML reframe) — access: **EASY** (single Zenodo DOI, direct download, free).
  - **BiGG Models database** (bigg.ucsd.edu) — ~80 manually curated GEMs across model organisms (E. coli, yeast, mouse, human, etc.) with full SBML3FBC + MetaNetX annotations — match: **CLOSE** for any downstream ML on metabolic models — access: **EASY** (free download, REST API).
  - **AGORA** (Magnúsdóttir et al. 2017 *Nat Biotechnol*) — 773 human-gut-microbiota GEMs, all public — match: **CLOSE** for microbiome-focused metabolic-modeling projects — access: **EASY**.
  - **CarveMe** (Machado et al. 2018 *Nucleic Acids Res*) — automated reconstruction tool + ~5,500 bacterial GEMs — match: **CLOSE** for bacterial-strain-design projects — access: **EASY**.
  - **KBase** (kbase.us, Argonne / DOE) — thousands of automatically-reconstructed GEMs + a full reconstruction-and-analysis platform — match: **CLOSE** for end-to-end-pipeline projects — access: **EASY** (free account).
- **Data access difficulty (for the paper's own dataset):** **Easy** — the entire 10,780-model dataset + per-model ~150-feature test-result vectors are on Zenodo with a single-click DOI and an Apache-2.0-licensed reproducible analysis pipeline. **Highest-tier data access of any paper in the entire survey.**
- **Tooling I would need to learn:** **Heaviest new-domain-tooling lift in the survey for a non-image / non-atomic-structure article.** Standard carry-over: pandas, NumPy, scikit-learn, LightGBM, PyTorch, matplotlib. **New systems-biology stack** (none of which appear in any prior professor's section):
  - **`cobrapy`** — Python constraint-based-modeling library (well-documented, mature; the canonical Python entry point to FBA / FVA / strain design)
  - **`libsbml` + SBML3FBC format** — XML-based standard for encoding metabolic models (steep but well-documented)
  - **`memote` itself** — to actually run the test suite on candidate models
  - **Constraint-based modeling concepts** — flux-balance analysis (LP), flux-variability analysis, biomass-objective formulations, Gibbs-free-energy thermodynamic constraints, GPR-rule semantics. The student's chemistry-undergrad background gives partial overlap (mass-balance / stoichiometry intuition transfers; LP optimisation does not).
  - **For the GNN reframe:** PyTorch Geometric on bipartite metabolite-reaction graphs (new, but well-documented).
  - **The systems-biology jargon density is the heaviest of any paper in the survey** — there is very little overlap with the chemistry-undergrad / RDKit / sklearn / Materials-Project ecosystems already established by Sánchez-Lengeling, Moosavi, Galatro, Beharry, Seferos, Frank Gu, and Edwards.
- **Portfolio angle — what a good GitHub project based on this would look like:** Three viable framings, in increasing order of ambition and tooling cost:
  1. **Lightest — tabular regression on the MEMOTE Zenodo deposit.** Download the 10,780-model × ~150-feature CSV from Zenodo, train a LightGBM regressor to predict overall MEMOTE score from the input feature subset; produce SHAP-value plots to rank tests by importance; cluster models with UMAP / t-SNE and characterise clusters by collection. README claim: *"Reproduces the t-SNE clustering reported by Lieven et al. 2020 and adds an interpretable ML model that flags which MEMOTE tests dominate the overall quality signal."* Tooling cost: low (sklearn / LightGBM / pandas only). Portfolio impact: moderate.
  2. **Medium — GEM-quality-from-organism-metadata recommender.** Pull each model's organism identifier, fetch genome metadata from NCBI Assembly / GTDB, train a regressor to predict MEMOTE score from organism descriptors. README claim: *"ML recommender flags low-quality automatically-built GEMs from organism metadata alone, before running the full MEMOTE test suite."* Tooling cost: low + one new API client (NCBI Assembly).
  3. **Heaviest — Graph neural network for missing-reaction prediction on metabolic graphs.** Reformulate each MEMOTE-validated GEM as a bipartite metabolite-reaction graph; mask a held-out subset of reactions per model; train a GNN (GCN / GAT / GraphSAGE) in PyTorch Geometric to predict the masked links; evaluate on cross-collection held-out splits. README claim: *"GNN-based gap-filler for genome-scale metabolic models, validated on the 10,780-GEM MEMOTE benchmark."* Tooling cost: heavy (`cobrapy` + `libsbml` + PyTorch Geometric + bipartite-graph plumbing). Portfolio impact: highest — connects to active research directions in the field and to Mahadevan's likely supervision interests.

### Article 2: Heavy Metal Removal by Bioaccumulation Using Genetically Engineered Microorganisms (BioZone Review Article)

- **Full citation:** Diep, P., **Mahadevan, R.**, & Yakunin, A. F. (2018). Heavy Metal Removal by Bioaccumulation Using Genetically Engineered Microorganisms. *Frontiers in Bioengineering and Biotechnology*, 6:157. DOI: 10.3389/fbioe.2018.00157. **Article type:** Review article, 3-author U of T-internal piece anchored at BioZone (Centre for Applied Biosciences and Bioengineering, Department of Chemical Engineering and Applied Chemistry).
- **Authorship caveat — important:** Patrick Diep is **first author** (U of T PhD student at BioZone) and **Alexander F. Yakunin is corresponding author** (`a.iakounine@utoronto.ca`). **Mahadevan is middle author (#2 of 3)**. The author-contributions statement is explicit: *"PD conceived, wrote, and approved the review for publication. RM and AY helped revise the manuscript for submission."* So Mahadevan's role here is **co-supervisor / reviser, not lead PI** — Yakunin is the senior corresponding author on this one. **Lab-signal is moderate** — better than Article 1's 60-author community-paper signatory situation (this is a 3-author U of T-internal paper with an explicit Mahadevan revising role), but still NOT Mahadevan-led. **Both Mahadevan articles processed so far have weak Mahadevan-specific authorship signal.** *Strongly recommend processing at least one Mahadevan-first-or-corresponding-author paper before treating the rollup as a stable advisor signal.*
- ⚠️ **Critical terminology clash to flag up front:** This article uses "**GEMs**" to mean **Genetically Engineered Microorganisms**. Mahadevan's Article 1 (the MEMOTE paper) uses "**GEMs**" to mean **Genome-Scale Metabolic Models**. **Same acronym, completely different meaning.** When reading both Mahadevan papers consecutively, this is a real source of confusion — flag it explicitly. The two senses of "GEMs" are arguably both within Mahadevan's research domain (he uses metabolic models to design engineered microorganisms), but they are *distinct* acronym uses in *distinct* sub-fields.
- **Problem in plain English:** Wastewater from mines, metal smelters, and oil/gas refineries is laden with toxic heavy metal ions (HMs: Cd, Co, Cu, Hg, Ni, As, U). Conventional removal techniques (chemical precipitation, activated carbon, ion exchange) generate sludge waste, use non-renewable adsorbents, and cannot reliably hit the new strict regulatory limits (1.2–5 µg L⁻¹ for aquatic life). **Microbial bioaccumulation** uses metabolically-active engineered microbes to **import** HMs across the cell membrane via transporter proteins (channels, secondary carriers, primary active transporters — Table 2 of the paper) and **sequester** them inside the cell using metal-binding proteins like metallothioneins, phytochelatins (peptide chains synthesised by phytochelatin synthase), and polyphosphates (Tables 3 and 4 of the paper). The review consolidates ~50 published bioaccumulation studies across 20+ years into a biochemical framework, identifies recurring strategies that worked and didn't, and proposes pivoting from "bioremediation" (just removing the metals) to **bioextraction** (recovering valuable metals such as gold, platinum, and strategic metals from waste streams as the "mine of tomorrow"). Industrial adoption has been "virtually non-existent" despite 25+ years of academic work.
- **ML task type:** **No ML in the paper** (it's a review). The most ML-portfolio-relevant artifacts are **Tables 1 (~50 bioaccumulation studies with measured capacities)**, **Table 2 (importer proteins by transporter class with UniProt IDs)**, **Table 3 (~30 metal-binding storage proteins with UniProt IDs)**, and **Table 4 (phytochelatin-synthesis enzymes with UniProt IDs)**. Several natural ML reframes:
  1. **Tabular regression on Table 1.** Predict bioaccumulation capacity Q_m (mg_HM g_DW⁻¹) from features: HM type (Cd / Co / Cu / Hg / Ni / As / U / multi-metal), chassis (E. coli vs S. cerevisiae vs M. huakuii vs D. radiodurans vs ...), strategy (import-only / storage-only / both), starting concentration, time, transporter class. ~50 rows directly digestible from Table 1.
  2. **Protein-sequence ML using the ~30 UniProt IDs in Tables 2 and 3.** Fetch sequences via UniProt REST API, train a classifier (or fine-tune a pretrained protein language model — **ESM-2** from Meta AI, **ProtBERT** from Rostlab) to predict "metal binder vs not" or specifically "Cd binder vs Hg binder vs Ni binder vs negative control." Direct connection to the protein-language-model field which is a hot ML topic in 2024–2026.
  3. **Literature-mining extension** across the ~70 cited primary papers to expand Table 1 to ~150–200 rows.
  4. **Cross-link with Article 1** — combine MEMOTE-validated GEMs (Article 1's sense) with bioaccumulation strain-design data (Article 2's sense) into a unified portfolio. This is the strongest cross-paper narrative within Mahadevan's section so far.
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality (paper): Tabulated literature data — Table 1 has ~50 entries × ~7 columns; Tables 2, 3, 4 have UniProt IDs + organism + target HM. PDB structures referenced in Fig. 1 (1LDA, 4GBY, 3J09, 2OMF, 1T2Y).
  - Modality (tabular ML reframe): ~7 features per row from Table 1 (HM, chassis, strategy, conc_in, conc_ads, time, transporter class).
  - Modality (protein-sequence ML reframe): amino-acid strings of ~30 importer/storage proteins from Tables 2, 3, 4 (typical lengths: 5 kDa metallothioneins ~50 aa, transporters ~300–500 aa, phytochelatin synthases ~500 aa).
  - Shape / size if stated (paper): Table 1 = ~50 bioaccumulation studies; Tables 2–4 = ~30 distinct UniProt-cataloged proteins.
  - Preprocessing in the paper: none — it's a review.
- **Output representation:**
  - What the model would predict (ML reframe): scalar Q_m (mg g⁻¹) for the tabular angle; binary "metal binder vs not" or multi-class "Cd / Hg / Ni / Cu / As binder" for the protein-sequence angle.
  - Units / label space: Q_m ∈ ~[0.01, 10700] mg g⁻¹ across Table 1 (the upper range is the U-bioprecipitating *D. radiodurans* engineered strain).
- **Model architecture (brief):** No ML in the paper. Portfolio reframes: LightGBM / random-forest regressor on tabular Table-1 features; **Hugging Face ESM-2** (650M params, free download from Meta AI) or **ProtBERT** (Rostlab, similar profile) for the protein-sequence reframe — both pretrained on UniRef50 and fine-tunable on the ~30 sequences from Tables 2 and 3 with standard PyTorch.
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: Curated literature compilation across ~70 cited primary references.
  - Size: Table 1 = ~50 rows; Tables 2 + 3 + 4 = ~30 protein UniProt IDs.
  - Source: Compiled by Diep & co-authors from cited literature.
  - Public? **Tables themselves are public in the published PDF (CC-BY 4.0 — the paper itself is open access on Frontiers); UniProt IDs link to public UniProt sequences; PDB structures are public.** No separate consolidated CSV deposit and no code release.
  - Link: https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/fbioe.2018.00157 (open access).
- **Reported headline metric:** Reviews don't report a single supervised-ML metric. Headline empirical findings from Table 1: highest reported bioaccumulation capacity is **10,700 mg_U g_DW⁻¹** by *Deinococcus radiodurans* engineered with PhoK alkaline phosphatase (Kulkarni et al. 2013); for Cd, top performance is ~63 mg_Cd g_DW⁻¹ (E. coli JM109 engineered with CdtB importer + MT, Deng et al. 2007); for Hg, top is ~178 mg_Hg g_DW⁻¹ (E. coli XL1-blue, Kiyono et al. 2003); for Ni, ~60 mg_Ni g_DW⁻¹ (Deng et al. 2013).
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**
  - **Hand-digitisation of Table 1** (~50 rows) — match: **EXACT** (it *is* the dataset for the paper-internal tabular reframe) — access: **EASY** (one PDF table).
  - **UniProt REST API + Biopython** for the ~30 protein sequences in Tables 2, 3, 4 — match: **EXACT** for the protein-sequence reframe — access: **EASY** (free, well-documented, one-line `Bio.ExPASy.ScanProsite` or `requests.get` per UniProt ID).
  - **Hugging Face Hub pretrained protein language models** (ESM-2, ProtBERT, ProtT5) — match: **CLOSE** (state-of-the-art protein representations, pretrained on UniRef50, free download) — access: **EASY** (`from transformers import EsmModel`).
  - **PDB** for the 5 reference structures (1LDA, 4GBY, 3J09, 2OMF, 1T2Y) cited in Fig. 1 — match: **CLOSE** for any structure-based protein-engineering project — access: **EASY** (free, RCSB PDB).
  - **Cross-link with Article 1's MEMOTE Zenodo deposit** for a unified portfolio — match: **CLOSE** if you frame the project as "ML on Mahadevan-relevant bioengineering data" — access: **EASY** (already in the survey).
- **Data access difficulty (for the paper's own dataset):** **Easy** for the tabular Table-1 reframe; **easy** for the protein-sequence reframe via UniProt REST API. Lighter than Article 1's MEMOTE GNN reframe but heavier than just hand-digitising one table.
- **Tooling I would need to learn:** Standard carry-over: pandas, NumPy, scikit-learn, LightGBM, PyTorch, matplotlib. **New for the protein-sequence reframe:** Hugging Face `transformers` library + ESM-2 / ProtBERT models (well-documented, free Colab tutorials available; this is a real domain skill that transfers across the survey to potential future bio-ML papers); **Biopython + UniProt REST API** (standard bioinformatics, well-documented). **No need for cobrapy / libsbml / SBML3FBC** (substantially lighter than Article 1's MEMOTE-based reframes). The tooling profile is closer to Sánchez-Lengeling Article 1 than to Article 1 of this section.
- **Portfolio angle — what a good GitHub project based on this would look like:** Two viable framings:
  1. **Lightest — tabular regression on Table 1.** Hand-transcribe the ~50 bioaccumulation studies from Table 1 into a CSV; train a LightGBM regressor + a Gaussian-process baseline to predict Q_m from (HM type, chassis, strategy, starting concentration, transporter class, time); produce a SHAP-value plot ranking which features matter most for capacity. README claim: *"Reproducible ML benchmark for engineered-microorganism heavy-metal bioaccumulation capacity, built on the curated literature in Diep, Mahadevan & Yakunin 2018."* Tooling cost: low (sklearn / LightGBM / pandas only). Portfolio impact: moderate.
  2. **Stretch — protein-language-model classifier for metal-binding proteins.** Fetch the ~30 UniProt sequences from Tables 2 + 3 + 4 (importers + storage proteins + phytochelatin enzymes); fine-tune ESM-2 (650M params, free) on the binary task "metal binder vs random UniRef50 negative control" or the multi-class task "what HM does this protein bind?" Use the protein language model's per-residue attention to highlight metal-binding residues (cysteines for MTs, histidines for Hpn-style storage proteins); compare against the literature annotations in Table 3. README claim: *"Pretrained ESM-2 fine-tuned on the curated metal-binding protein set from Diep, Mahadevan & Yakunin 2018 reproduces the literature-annotated metal-binding residue motifs from sequence alone."* Tooling cost: low + one new domain library (Hugging Face `transformers`); portfolio impact: high (protein language models are recruiter-hot in 2024–2026 and this connects environmental engineering to AI-for-biology).

### Article 3: EMILiO — A Fast Algorithm for Genome-Scale Strain Design (FIRST Mahadevan-Corresponding-Author Paper in This Section)

- **Full citation:** Yang, L., Cluett, W. R., & **Mahadevan, R.** (2011). EMILiO: A fast algorithm for genome-scale strain design. *Metabolic Engineering*, 13, 272–281. DOI: 10.1016/j.ymben.2011.03.002. **Article type:** Primary research paper in *Metabolic Engineering* (top-tier systems-biology / metabolic-engineering journal).
- **Authorship — finally a clean Mahadevan lab signal:** Laurence Yang is **first author** (Mahadevan's PhD student at U of T Chemical Engineering and Applied Chemistry); William R. Cluett is middle author (U of T Chem Eng faculty co-supervisor); **Mahadevan is corresponding author** (`krishna.mahadevan@utoronto.ca`). Three-author U-of-T-only paper. **This is the first Mahadevan-CORRESPONDING-author paper processed in this section** — it resolves the open-items concern from Articles 1 and 2 that "neither article is Mahadevan-led" and confirms the previously-inferred research direction (computational strain design + constraint-based modeling + bilevel optimization for metabolic engineering). **Strongest Mahadevan-specific lab signal in the section.**
- **Beautiful unification of the two earlier articles in Mahadevan's section:** EMILiO **uses genome-scale metabolic models** (Article 1's "GEMs" sense — the iAF1260 *E. coli* model from Feist et al. 2007) to **design genetically engineered microorganisms** (Article 2's "GEMs" sense — succinate / L-glutamate / L-serine over-producing *E. coli* strains). The acronym clash flagged in Articles 1 and 2 is resolved by Article 3: both senses of "GEM" are tied together through Mahadevan's actual research programme, where genome-scale metabolic models are the *substrate* for designing engineered microorganisms.
- **Problem in plain English:** Metabolic engineers want to genetically modify microbes (typically *E. coli* or yeast) to over-produce target chemicals — succinate (~$10B-market biopolymer precursor), amino acids (L-glutamate, L-serine), biofuels, drug intermediates. With ~2000 reactions in a genome-scale *E. coli* model, choosing even 5 modifications gives ~8 trillion combinations; choosing the *flux level* of each modified reaction adds continuous-search complexity on top. Existing algorithms (**OptKnock** 2003 for knockouts only, **OptReg** 2006 for ±regulation, **GDLS** 2009 for local search) had two limitations: (1) only discrete modifications (delete / overexpress / suppress), but Alper et al. 2005 showed lycopene production in *E. coli* is maximized at *intermediate* dxs expression — not full deletion or full overexpression; (2) the underlying mixed-integer linear programs become combinatorially expensive for >3 modifications. **EMILiO** (*E*nhancing *M*etabolism with *I*terative *L*inear *O*ptimization) solves both: (a) handles continuous flux levels by importing **successive linear programming (SLP)** from the petrochemical industry's 50-year-old toolbox; (b) reformulates the bilevel optimization as a single-level mathematical program with complementarity constraints (MPCC) using KKT conditions; (c) uses a 3-stage pipeline (SLP → recursive LP-pruning → MILP) to extract minimal sets of modifications. Result: 100% maximal succinate production with 3 modifications in **2 minutes CPU time**, vs OptReg* taking 4 days to reach 83.26% and OptReg*LS local-search taking 4 hours for 82.13%.
- **ML task type:** **No supervised ML in the paper.** The paper is constrained-optimization (LP + MILP + bilevel + MPCC + KKT) plus **affinity-propagation clustering** (Frey & Dueck 2007, unsupervised) of the 234 strain designs in Fig. 5. ML reframes for a portfolio project:
  1. **Tabular regression on the 234-strain-design output dataset.** Predict succinate production (% of theoretical maximum) from a feature vector encoding which of ~100+ reactions are modified and how (knockout / up-regulate to flux X / down-regulate to flux Y). Cleanest supervised setup of any Mahadevan article processed so far. Could also reproduce + extend Fig. 3's "landscape of strain designs" using t-SNE / UMAP / hierarchical clustering instead of affinity propagation.
  2. **Reinforcement learning / Bayesian optimization for strain design.** Replace EMILiO's SLP/MPCC formulation with a learned policy (PPO / SAC) that proposes strain modifications and uses cobrapy + iAF1260 as the simulator/reward. This is genuinely an active research direction in 2024–2026 (e.g., learned bilevel-optimization for metabolic engineering, AlphaFold-style search over strain-design space).
  3. **FBA-surrogate neural network.** Train a feed-forward NN (or graph NN on the metabolite-reaction bipartite graph from Article 1) to predict FBA outputs (max growth rate, max product flux) from input modifications + media conditions, eliminating most of the inner LP solves during exploration. Speeds up RL-style strain design by ~100×.
  4. **Cross-paper unification** with Articles 1 and 2. **Strongest narrative:** use MEMOTE-validated GEMs (Article 1) as the model substrate → run EMILiO-style strain-design (Article 3) targeting heavy-metal bioaccumulation (Article 2) → fine-tune ESM-2 (Article 2) on the resulting predicted-overexpressed metal-binding proteins. **All three Mahadevan articles connect through cobrapy + the iAF1260 model + strain design** — this section is the strongest within-section narrative arc of any professor in the survey aside from Frank Gu's Article 3 → Article 1 review-then-experiment pair.
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality (paper): `iAF1260` genome-scale model of *E. coli* (Feist et al. 2007) — ~1260 ORFs, ~2000 reactions, stoichiometric matrix S ∈ ℝ^{m×n}, GPR rules, biomass objective. Plus media conditions (max glucose / oxygen uptake = 20 mmol/gDW/h), minimum growth rate (0.1 h⁻¹), restricted target-reaction set (excluding cell-envelope / glycerophospholipid / transport reactions per Feist et al. 2010).
  - Modality (ML reframe via tabular outputs): each of the 234 strain designs in Dataset S2 = ~100-dim binary/continuous feature vector (which reactions modified + their target flux levels) + scalar target (succinate% / L-glutamate% / L-serine% of maximum).
  - Modality (ML reframe via RL): state = current (set-of-modifications + iAF1260 model); action = add/remove a modification with a flux level; reward = FBA-predicted product flux at max growth.
  - Shape / size if stated (paper): 234 strain designs for succinate; additional designs for L-glutamate (multiple) and L-serine (multiple, including a 99.65%-maximal design with non-intuitive PPA / PPKr modifications).
  - Preprocessing in the paper: model reduction via reduced row echelon form of S; restriction of target-reaction set; conversion of bilevel optimization → MPCC via KKT; iterative SLP with a single tunable parameter K_p (objective weight on product maximization).
- **Output representation:**
  - What the model predicts (paper): set of reactions to modify + how (knockout vs continuous flux level v_L / v_U) + predicted % of maximum product flux.
  - What the model would predict (ML reframe): scalar product-flux% from tabular reframe; modification policy from RL reframe; FBA outputs from surrogate-NN reframe.
  - Units / label space: product-flux% ∈ [0, 100]; flux levels in mmol/gDW/h (succinate v_max = 32.25 mmol/gDW/h for the iAF1260 baseline).
- **Model architecture (brief):** No ML. EMILiO = 3-stage pipeline: (1) **Successive Linear Programming (SLP)** to identify large set of active flux-bound constraints; (2) recursive **LP-based pruning** to find subsets of constraints that achieve user-defined production threshold; (3) final **MILP** minimizing number of modifications. Implemented in MATLAB with CPLEX 11.2. ML reframes: LightGBM / XGBoost for tabular regression; Stable-Baselines3 PPO / SAC for RL; small MLP or PyTorch Geometric GraphSAGE for FBA-surrogate.
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: 234 EMILiO-generated strain designs for succinate production + supplementary L-glutamate / L-serine designs.
  - Size: 234 succinate strains (Dataset S2) + ~5–10 amino-acid strains (Dataset S4) + literature comparison (Dataset S3) + per-strain flux deviation vectors (Dataset S1).
  - Source: Generated by EMILiO using the public `iAF1260` model from Feist et al. 2007 (also public on BiGG).
  - Public? **YES** — Datasets S1, S2, S3, S4 are journal supplementary information, openly downloadable from the *Metabolic Engineering* article page. The `iAF1260` model itself is on BiGG (free download). MATLAB / CPLEX implementation is described in detail in the paper's Methods + a cited follow-up (Yang et al. 2010 Symposium proceedings) but **no GitHub code release** (this is a 2011 paper, before GitHub-as-deposit became standard).
  - Link: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ymben.2011.03.002 (paper + supplementary); BiGG = http://bigg.ucsd.edu/models/iAF1260.
- **Reported headline metric:** **EMILiO finds 100% maximal succinate production with 3 modifications (SUCDi knockout + FRD2 up-regulation + aconitase up-regulation) in 2 minutes CPU time on a 3.2 GHz Intel Xeon.** OptReg* (modified reference algorithm with the same objective) terminated after 4 days at 83.26%; OptReg*LS local-search reached 82.13% in 4 hours. Across the 234 alternate succinate strains: range 75–100% maximal production, 15 affinity-propagation clusters distinguished by which subset of citric-acid-cycle / glyoxylate-shunt / acetate-metabolism reactions are modified. L-glutamate: 100% maximal flux strain found (knockout AKGDH + GLUDC + activate forward ICDH). L-serine: 99.65% maximal flux strain found (using non-intuitive PPA / PPKr modifications).
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**
  - **EMILiO supplementary datasets S1–S4** — match: **EXACT** for the tabular-regression reframe — access: **EASY** (journal supplementary download).
  - **`iAF1260` model on BiGG** — match: **EXACT** for any reimplementation in cobrapy — access: **EASY** (free, single download, MIT-licensed).
  - **Cross-link with Article 1's MEMOTE Zenodo deposit** (10,780 GEMs incl. iAF1260 itself) — match: **CLOSE** for unified portfolio — access: **EASY** (already in survey).
  - **OptKnock paper data** (Burgard et al. 2003 *Biotechnol Bioeng*) — earlier work, public — match: **CLOSE** for any "evolution-of-strain-design-algorithms" historical project — access: **EASY**.
  - **`cobrapy` itself** with example strain-design tutorials — match: **CLOSE** for reimplementing EMILiO in modern Python — access: **EASY** (well-documented, MIT-licensed).
- **Data access difficulty (for the paper's own dataset):** **Easy** — Datasets S1–S4 are supplementary downloads; iAF1260 is on BiGG. **One step easier than Article 2** (which needs Table 1 hand-transcription) but with the **same tooling-lift cost as Article 1** (cobrapy + libsbml + LP/MILP solver) if you want to actually run / extend EMILiO rather than just analyze its outputs.
- **Tooling I would need to learn:** **Same systems-biology stack as Article 1** — cobrapy, libsbml, SBML3FBC, FBA / FVA, constraint-based-modeling concepts. **Plus mathematical optimization concepts new to the chemistry student:** linear programming, mixed-integer linear programming, bilevel optimization, KKT conditions, MPCCs. Free academic licenses: CPLEX (IBM), Gurobi (free academic), GLPK (open-source). For ML reframe: pandas / sklearn / LightGBM (familiar) for tabular; Stable-Baselines3 + Gym for RL; PyTorch Geometric for FBA-surrogate GNN. **No avoiding the cobrapy stack here** (unlike Article 2, which had a cobrapy-free protein-sequence reframe). Heaviest tooling lift of the three Mahadevan articles, tied with Article 1.
- **Portfolio angle — what a good GitHub project based on this would look like:** Three viable framings, in increasing order of ambition:
  1. **Lightest — tabular regression on Dataset S2.** Download Dataset S2 (234 succinate strain designs); convert each strain's modifications into a feature vector; train LightGBM / XGBoost to predict succinate% from modifications; produce SHAP plots ranking which reactions matter most for production; reproduce the affinity-propagation clusters from Fig. 5 using UMAP / t-SNE / hierarchical clustering. README claim: *"Reproducible ML benchmark on the EMILiO 234-strain-design dataset, recovering the citric-acid-cycle / glyoxylate-shunt cluster structure reported by Yang, Cluett & Mahadevan 2011 from a learned regressor."* Tooling cost: low (sklearn / LightGBM only).
  2. **Medium — reimplement EMILiO in cobrapy.** Port the SLP / MPCC / MILP pipeline from MATLAB+CPLEX to Python+cobrapy+Gurobi-or-GLPK; verify reproduction of the 100%-maximal succinate strain in <2 minutes; release on GitHub as `emilio-py`. README claim: *"Open-source Python reimplementation of the EMILiO strain-design algorithm (Yang, Cluett & Mahadevan 2011), reproducing the 100%-maximal succinate strain in cobrapy + Gurobi."* Tooling cost: medium-heavy (cobrapy + Gurobi/GLPK + bilevel optimization concepts). Portfolio impact: high — recruiter-legible to synthetic-biology / Ginkgo-Bioworks-style audiences.
  3. **Heaviest — RL-replacement for EMILiO.** Frame strain design as a sequential-decision-making problem; train a PPO or SAC policy that proposes modifications using cobrapy + iAF1260 as the simulator/reward; benchmark against EMILiO on succinate / L-glutamate / L-serine; ablate by removing the FBA simulator and replacing with a learned surrogate NN. README claim: *"Reinforcement-learning replacement for EMILiO matches the 100%-maximal succinate target on the iAF1260 model with 10× faster wall-clock time once the surrogate is trained."* Tooling cost: heaviest (cobrapy + Stable-Baselines3 + PyTorch + FBA-surrogate NN training). Portfolio impact: highest — bridges metabolic engineering + RL, both individually recruiter-hot.

**Article 3 verdict:** **The strongest Mahadevan article in the section** for both authorship signal and portfolio impact. ### Summary for this professor

- **Common thread across their work (based on Articles 1, 2, and 3):** **Computational strain design + constraint-based metabolic modeling for synthetic biology / metabolic engineering**, now **confirmed by Article 3** (the first Mahadevan-corresponding-author paper). The three articles span Mahadevan's research programme cleanly: Article 1 = the bioinformatics infrastructure (10,780-GEM MEMOTE quality-control benchmark) that ensures genome-scale models are usable; Article 2 = an application domain (heavy-metal bioaccumulation in engineered microbes) that motivates strain-design work; Article 3 = the **algorithmic core** (EMILiO computational strain design via successive linear programming + bilevel optimization + KKT reformulation, Yang–Cluett–Mahadevan 2011). **The "GEMs" acronym clash that confused Articles 1 and 2 is resolved by Article 3:** EMILiO **uses genome-scale metabolic models** (Article 1's "GEMs") to **design genetically engineered microorganisms** (Article 2's "GEMs"). Both senses of GEM are real, both fall within Mahadevan's research domain, and Article 3 is the bridge.
- **Dominant data modalities:** (i) Genome-scale metabolic models in SBML3FBC format with stoichiometric matrices + GPR rules + biomass objectives + annotations (Articles 1 and 3 — both use BiGG-style models, Article 1 in aggregate across 7 collections, Article 3 specifically the iAF1260 *E. coli* model); (ii) literature-curated tabular bioaccumulation-capacity data + ~30 UniProt-cataloged metal-binding protein sequences (Article 2); (iii) **234-strain-design tabular dataset with modifications → product-flux% supervised structure (Article 3, the cleanest ML-friendly modality of the three articles).** Article 2's protein-sequence reframe avoids the cobrapy stack; Articles 1 and 3 require it.
- **Biggest obstacle for me to replicate their work publicly:** **Article-dependent.** Articles 1 and 3 demand the heaviest new-domain tooling lift in the survey (cobrapy + libsbml + SBML3FBC + flux-balance-analysis + LP/MILP optimization for Article 3); Article 2 is substantially lighter (sklearn / LightGBM + Hugging Face transformers for ESM-2 fine-tuning, no cobrapy required). **Article 3's Dataset S2 (234 strain designs) is the cleanest ML-friendly substrate of the three articles**, and the 6.9 score for Article 3 is the highest of the three for portfolio-impact (8 vs 7 for the other two). **Pick Article 3 as the anchor for any serious Mahadevan-inspired portfolio project; use Article 2 if the student wants to avoid the cobrapy tooling lift.**
- **Skills I would gain by working with them:** **From Article 1:** constraint-based metabolic modeling (FBA, FVA, GPR semantics, biomass-objective formulations); SBML3FBC + cobrapy fluency; bioinformatics quality-assurance tooling. **From Article 2:** Heavy-metal bioremediation / bioextraction concepts; transmembrane transporter biology; metallothionein / phytochelatin / polyphosphate biochemistry; protein-language-model fluency (ESM-2, ProtBERT). **From Article 3:** **Computational strain design + bilevel optimization + KKT conditions + LP/MILP/MPCC mathematical-programming formulations** — these are the *core* skills of the metabolic-engineering / synthetic-biology field, and Article 3 confirms they are exactly what Mahadevan teaches his trainees. Plus affinity-propagation / unsupervised clustering on strain-design landscapes. **Strong industry-relevant skill set for synthetic-biology recruiters (Ginkgo Bioworks, Amyris, Zymergen, Pivot Bio, LanzaTech, Sublime Systems, Genentech computational-biology groups), plus environmental-engineering / mining-bioextraction startups for the Article-2 angle.**

### Open items / notes

- Lab webpage / Google Scholar URL for Prof. Mahadevan not yet confirmed — verify before finalising.
- Personal interest score for Article 1 not yet provided by student.
- Personal interest score for Article 2 not yet provided by student.
- Personal interest score for Article 3 not yet provided by student.
- **Authorship pattern across the three processed Mahadevan articles** (now resolved with Article 3): Article 1 = ~60-author openCOBRA-consortium community paper, Mahadevan signatory #22 (NOT first / last / corresponding); Article 2 = 3-author U of T-internal review, Mahadevan MIDDLE author (Yakunin is corresponding); **Article 3 = 3-author U of T-only primary research paper, Mahadevan IS corresponding author** with PhD student Yang as first author. **Article 3 resolves the open-items concern from Articles 1 and 2 that "neither article is Mahadevan-led"** — Article 3 *is* Mahadevan-led, and it confirms the previously-inferred research direction (computational strain design + constraint-based modeling + bilevel optimization for metabolic engineering).
- ⚠️ **Acronym-clash resolution (now provided by Article 3):** Article 1's "GEMs" = Genome-Scale Metabolic Models; Article 2's "GEMs" = Genetically Engineered Microorganisms; **Article 3 = the bridge** — EMILiO uses genome-scale metabolic models (Article 1's GEMs) as input to design genetically engineered microorganisms (Article 2's GEMs). The acronym clash is real but the underlying concepts are unified through Mahadevan's actual research programme.
- **Lab-direction confirmed by Article 3:** computational strain design + constraint-based modeling + bilevel optimization for metabolic engineering of microbes producing succinate, amino acids (L-glutamate, L-serine), and (by extension to Article 2) heavy-metal-bioaccumulating strains. **Mahadevan's group works on the algorithmic + computational side of synthetic biology**, with downstream applications in biochemicals (Article 3) and bioremediation (Article 2), supported by community quality-assurance infrastructure (Article 1). This is a coherent and recruiter-legible research programme.
- **Eight viable portfolio paths** are now scoped across Articles 1, 2, and 3 at very different tooling costs:
  1. Article 1 — tabular regression on MEMOTE Zenodo features (~150 features × 10,780 models, low tooling cost on top of cobrapy)
  2. Article 1 — GEM-quality-from-organism-metadata recommender (low + NCBI Assembly API)
  3. Article 1 — GNN for missing-reaction prediction on bipartite metabolite-reaction graphs (high tooling cost)
  4. Article 2 — tabular regression on Table 1 bioaccumulation capacities (~50 rows, lowest tooling cost — no cobrapy)
  5. Article 2 — ESM-2 / ProtBERT fine-tuning on the ~30 metal-binding-protein UniProt IDs in Tables 2–4 (low + Hugging Face transformers, no cobrapy)
  6. Article 3 — tabular regression on Dataset S2's 234 strain designs (cleanest supervised setup of any Mahadevan article, low ML cost but cobrapy needed for any extension)
  7. Article 3 — Python reimplementation of EMILiO using cobrapy + Gurobi/GLPK (medium-heavy tooling, GitHub-portfolio-legible)
  8. Article 3 — RL-replacement for EMILiO using cobrapy + Stable-Baselines3 (heaviest tooling, highest portfolio impact)
  **The strongest single recommendation is now Path 6 or Path 7** (Article 3-anchored), because Article 3 is Mahadevan-corresponding-author + the 234-strain dataset is the cleanest supervised structure + portfolio impact is highest (8 vs 7 for Articles 1 and 2). **Alternative: a unified Articles 1+2+3 portfolio** combining MEMOTE-validated GEMs (Article 1) → EMILiO-style strain design (Article 3) targeting heavy-metal bioaccumulation (Article 2) → ESM-2 fine-tuning on resulting metal-binding proteins (Article 2) is the strongest within-section narrative arc and ties the GEMs-acronym clash together.
- **Cross-survey synergy worth flagging:** The MEMOTE 10,780-model Zenodo deposit (Article 1) pairs with **Sánchez-Lengeling Article 3 (MOSES generative-chemistry benchmark)** — both are large-scale community-curated datasets. The Article 2 ESM-2 / ProtBERT framing pairs with **Edwards Article 3 (90 α/β-hydrolase polyester degradation)** — both are protein-sequence ML on small curated bio datasets. **Article 3 (EMILiO) pairs with Edwards Article 2 (PaCE wastewater-treatment regression)** — both are tabular small-data regression on engineered-bioprocess outputs. A "computational-biology / engineered-microorganism bridge" portfolio combining Mahadevan's three articles with Edwards's three articles would be the strongest cross-professor narrative in the survey for a synthetic-biology / bio-ML recruiter audience.
- Confirm whether Mahadevan currently supervises master's theses at U of T (not just PhDs / postdocs) — at long-tenured U of T faculty status with confirmed PhD-supervision history (Yang in Article 3), master's supervision is plausible but should be verified.
